\paragraph{Portable source edition.}
Every TeX input used by this entry is expanded below. Historical file
and directory names in the preserved text are source locators; the
exact origin paths and hashes are recorded in \texttt{11\_SOURCE\_MAP.json}.
The other numbered proof files in this flat handoff supply the
accompanying complete calculations.\par

This continuation retains the original packet order $k$, the original
dimension $q=[1+k(m-1)](k+1)^2$, the source coordinate $S=k/2+iy$,
all roots and all four endpoints. Every new proof is printed below.
The complete preceding source closure, including the positive recurrence,
source transport, equilibrium and mixed kernel--boundary maps, accompanies
this paper in \texttt{dependencies/cut21}.

The first part concerns the unchanged construction. The second part
preserves the calculations for a separately chosen Gamma order $K$,
with their exact comparison maps and their stated scope. The exploratory
choice $K=q+1$ is not a rule supplied by the original construction and
does not replace its canonical source. The intrinsic constructions in
the first part are derived from the original spaces and operators.

% BEGIN INLINE SOURCE 1; SHA256 120f4b905db4178fe5caf15fce02a81a183d76357c9a9409dc1d20b43186f681



This calculation continues the full-source comparison in BRD1--42 and
CTR1--54. It treats the original one-column relation determinant separately,
so the degree condition \(r\ge l\) of the projector construction is never
applied to the low endpoint. Every comparison below is on the original
scalar coefficient space, with the original polynomial multiplication
maps and all four tail integrals present. The complete BRD, FTC and CTR
sources supply the proved contour, polynomial and high-endpoint formulas.

\section{Objects and the low source maps}
Retain
\[
 k=4l+1,\quad l\ge1,\quad m\ge1,\quad e=1+k(m-1),\quad
 q=e(k+1)^2,\quad c=k/2,\quad 0<\delta<1/2,\quad\gamma>2.
 \tag{LET1}
\]
Set \(d=\delta^2,\ g=\gamma^2\), and define the complete original objects
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \chi(S)=\prod_{a,b=0}^k
 [S-c-(2a-k)\delta-i(2b-k)\gamma]^e,\\
 f(S)=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(S-c-t_j),\quad t_j=2j+\tfrac12,
 \qquad \sigma(y)=\frac{|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2}{2\pi}.
\end{gathered}\tag{LET2}
\]
Write \(m_j=\int_{\mathbb R}y^j\sigma(y)\,dy\), with the index denoting the
literal power of \(y\). Thus \(m_0=\sqrt{2\pi}\), odd moments vanish, and
CTR50--53 supply all even moments by their exact rational recurrence with
the full mass factor. The original low relation endpoint is
\[
 \mathfrak r_\chi=
 \frac{\int_{\mathbb R}|f(c+iy)\chi(c+iy)|^2\sigma(y)\,dy}
      {\int_{\mathbb R}|\chi(c+iy)|^2\sigma(y)\,dy}.
 \tag{LET3}
\]
All four scalar integrals used below are finite by the proved Gamma upper
bound and strictly positive because their polynomial factors vanish at
only finitely many real points.

Put \(\epsilon=2^{-10}\), and partition the line into
\(I=\{|y|<q\epsilon\}\), \(B=\{q\epsilon\le |y|\le64q\}\), and
\(F=\{|y|>64q\}\). Assume the explicit finite threshold
\[
 k\ge2048\sqrt{d+g}.
 \tag{LET4}
\]
Then \(q\ge k^2\) gives \(k\sqrt{d+g}/q\le\epsilon/2\).
Also \(t_{l-1}<k/2\) and \(\sqrt{d+g}>2\) give
\(t_{l-1}/q\le\epsilon\). This is the same original-parameter domain
already used for the complete high-endpoint comparison.

On the bulk Hilbert space \(L^2(B,\sigma(y)\,dy)\), the maps from the
one-dimensional coefficient space are
\[
 T_{\rm pow}u(y)=(iy)^q u,\qquad
 T_\chi u(y)=\chi(c+iy)u,\qquad
 (Uh)(y)=\frac{\chi(c+iy)}{(iy)^q}h(y).
 \tag{LET5}
\]
Their exact identities are \(UT_{\rm pow}=T_\chi\) and
\(UM_f=M_fU\), where \(M_f\) is multiplication by \(f(c+iy)\).
Both \(U\) and its inverse are bounded: the bulk is compact, \(y\ne0\)
there, and LET4 places every displacement root strictly inside its inner
radius. The squared modulus of \(U\) is the original density ratio.
Taking scalar Hilbert Grams proves the source comparisons below while
retaining the original arithmetic quotient and its multiplication maps.

\section{A scalar ratio transport retaining the degree \texorpdfstring{\(l\)}{l}}
Let \(w\) be a finite positive measure, \(F_0\ge0\) a measurable function
with \(0<\int F_0\,dw<\infty\), and let \(\psi\) be a bounded real
function. Define the two literal ratios
\[
 R_w=\frac{\int F_0\,dw}{\int dw},\qquad
 R_{e^\psi w}=\frac{\int F_0e^\psi\,dw}{\int e^\psi\,dw}.
\]
If \(a\le\psi\le b\) almost everywhere, positivity of the integrals gives
\[
 e^a\le\frac{\int F_0e^\psi\,dw}{\int F_0\,dw}\le e^b,\qquad
 e^a\le\frac{\int e^\psi\,dw}{\int dw}\le e^b.
\]
Division and logarithms therefore prove the exact finite estimate
\[
 \left|\log R_{e^\psi w}-\log R_w\right|\le b-a.
 \tag{LET6}
\]
No restriction on the degree of \(F_0=|f|^2\) enters this scalar proof.

Apply LET6 to
\(dw=1_B(y)|y|^{2q}\sigma(y)\,dy\) and \(F_0(y)=|f(c+iy)|^2\).
For \(y=qz\), the unchanged root product has the expansion
\[
 \psi_\chi(qz):=\log\frac{|\chi(c+iqz)|^2}{|qz|^{2q}}
 =\frac{a_k}{z^2}+E_\chi(qz),\quad
 a_k=\frac{k(k+2)(d-g)}{3q},\quad
 |E_\chi(qz)|\le\delta_\chi,
\]
\[
 \tau_\epsilon=\frac{k^2(d+g)}{q^2\epsilon^2}\le\tfrac14,\qquad
 \delta_\chi=\frac{q\tau_\epsilon^2}{2(1-\tau_\epsilon)}.
 \tag{LET7}
\]
For completeness, write each factor as
\(iqz(1+i\zeta_{ab}/(qz))\). The logarithmic Taylor series converges
absolutely; the permutation \((a,b)\mapsto(k-a,k-b)\) cancels odd powers.
The quadratic sum is
\(e\operatorname{Re}\sum_{a,b}\zeta_{ab}^2=
qk(k+2)(d-g)/3\). The remaining even powers have absolute sum at most
\(q\sum_{j\ge2}\tau_\epsilon^j/j
 \le q\tau_\epsilon^2/[2(1-\tau_\epsilon)]\).
This proves every coefficient and remainder in LET7.

Since \(z^{-2}\in[64^{-2},\epsilon^{-2}]\), define the finite constant
\[
 E_0=|a_k|(\epsilon^{-2}-64^{-2})+2\delta_\chi.
 \tag{LET8}
\]
\newpage
The exact bulk difference
\[
 d_\chi=
 \log\frac{\int_B|f\chi|^2d\sigma}{\int_B|\chi|^2d\sigma}
 -\log\frac{\int_B|f(c+iy)|^2|y|^{2q}d\sigma}
             {\int_B|y|^{2q}d\sigma}
\]
satisfies \(|d_\chi|\le E_0\) by LET6. All products in the first
ratio are evaluated at \(c+iy\).
The original numerator multiplication also gives
\[
 1\le \frac{|f(c+iy)|^2}{|y|^{2l}}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(1+t_j^2/y^2)\le e^{\Delta_f},
 \qquad
 \Delta_f=\frac{S_2}{q^2\epsilon^2},\quad
 S_2=\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}t_j^2
     =\frac{l(16l^2-12l-1)}{12}.
 \tag{LET9}
\]
The upper bound follows by integrating the inequality
\((\log(1+x))'=1/(1+x)\le1\) from zero. Expanding each \(t_j^2\) and
summing \(j,j^2\) gives the stated \(S_2\).
Consequently the logarithmic correction \(d_f\) obtained by replacing
the bulk numerator \(|f(c+iy)|^2|y|^{2q}\) by
\(|y|^{2q+2l}\) satisfies \(0\le d_f\le\Delta_f\), with that sign.

\section{The four scalar tails on the original line}
Consider the four positive integrals with weights
\[
 h_{\chi,0}=|\chi(c+iy)|^2,\quad
 h_{\chi,f}=|f(c+iy)\chi(c+iy)|^2,\quad
 h_{{\rm pow},0}=|y|^{2q},\quad
 h_{{\rm pow},l}=|y|^{2q+2l}.
\]
For each weight let \(H=\int h\,d\sigma=H^I+H^B+H^F\) and
\[
 t_H=\log\left(1+\frac{H^I+H^F}{H^B}\right)\ge0,\qquad
 \log H=\log H^B+t_H.
 \tag{LET10}
\]
Here every \(H^B>0\). We prove a common bound at scalar rank one rather
than applying a high-rank statement outside its specified domain.

The original contour and reflection calculation BRD23--24 proves
\[
 C_Le^{-\pi|y|}\le\sigma(y)\le C_Ue^{-|y|},\qquad
 C_L=\frac{\pi}{\Gamma(3/4)^2},\quad
 C_U=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{2\pi\sqrt{\cos1}},\quad
 C_U/C_L=(\cos1)^{-1/2}.
 \tag{LET11}
\]
Those proofs retain the Euler contour rotation and keyhole residue;
they are included in the complete source dependencies. Pairing the roots
of \(\chi\) as \(\zeta,-\zeta\) gives
\[
 |\chi(c+iy)|^2\le(y^2+k^2(d+g))^q.
\]
On \(1\le z\le2\), the reverse triangle inequality instead gives
\(|\chi(c+iqz)|^2\ge q^{2q}(3/4)^q\), and
\(|f(c+iqz)|^2\ge q^{2l}\).
On \(|z|\le\epsilon\), LET4 gives the respective upper bounds
\(q^{2q}(2\epsilon^2)^q\) and \(q^{2l}(2\epsilon^2)^l\).
The coefficient polynomial is exactly the constant one. In particular
its scalar value is bounded by \(8^{2q}\int_1^2 1\,dz\), with no
missing coefficient directions. Integrating over the inner segment and
dividing by the positive bulk subsegment gives for both actual weights
\[
 H^I/H^B\le\kappa_{I,1}
 :=\frac{2\epsilon}{\sqrt{\cos1}}
   \left(\frac43\,2^{-13}e^{2\pi}\right)^q.
 \tag{LET12}
\]
Indeed the numerator/denominator ratio before enlarging it is
\(2\epsilon(\cos1)^{-1/2}
 (4/3)^q e^{2\pi q}(2\epsilon^2)^{q+s}\), \(s=0,l\).
Multiplication by \(8^{2q}\ge1\) gives LET12 because
\(2\epsilon^2\,8^2=2^{-13}\) and
\((2\epsilon^2)^s\le1\).
For the reference powers the corresponding stronger constant is
\(2\epsilon(\cos1)^{-1/2}\epsilon^{2s}
 (2^{-14}e^{2\pi})^q\), hence is at most LET12.

For the far estimate retain \(D_s=2q+2s\le5q\).
At \(z\ge64\), integration of
\(\log(z/64)\le(z-64)/64\) gives
\[
 \int_{64}^{\infty}z^{D_s}e^{-qz}dz
 \le\frac{64^{D_s}e^{-64q}}{q-D_s/64},
 \qquad q-D_s/64\ge59q/64.
 \tag{LET13}
\]
For the actual weights, LET7 on the bulk subsegment gives the lower
factor \(e^{-\eta}\), where
\(\eta=-a_k+\delta_\chi(1)\), and
\(\delta_\chi(x)=q\tau_x^2/[2(1-\tau_x)]\).
The far upper weights have the extra factors
\(e^{\delta_\chi(64)+\Delta_{f,s}(64)}\), where
\(\Delta_{f,0}=0\), \(\Delta_{f,l}(x)=S_2/(q^2x^2)\).
Using LET11, both real tails, and the Jacobian \(dy=q\,dz\), their
ratio is at most
\[
 \frac{2}{\sqrt{\cos1}}\,
 \frac{e^{2\pi q+\eta+\delta_\chi(64)+\Delta_{f,s}(64)}
             64^{D_s}e^{-64q}}{q-D_s/64}.
 \tag{LET14}
\]
No polynomial-growth factor is needed for the constant coefficient.
The original domain implies \(\tau_1\le1/4\) and \(k\ge5\).
It gives separately
\[
 -a_k\le7q/60,\quad \delta_\chi(1)\le q/24,\quad
 \delta_\chi(64)\le q/24,\quad
 \Delta_{f,l}(64)\le q/8192.
\]
The last follows from \(S_2\le4l^3\) and \(2l\le q\).
Their sum is less than \(q/4\).
Using \(64-2\pi-5\log64>20\), LET13--14 therefore give
\[
 H^F/H^B\le \frac{128}{59q\sqrt{\cos1}}e^{-19q}
 \le\kappa_F:=\frac{512q}{59\sqrt{\cos1}}e^{-19q}.
 \tag{LET15}
\]
The elementary displayed strict exponent bound follows from
\(\pi<4\) and \(\log2<1\). For the reference powers all three correction
factors in LET14 equal one, so the same inequality follows directly.
Thus the four actual scalar corrections obey
\[
 0\le t_H\le L_1:=\log(1+\kappa_{I,1}+\kappa_F).
 \tag{LET16}
\]
The constant \(\kappa_{I,1}\) decays exponentially: as proved by the
positive series for \(\log2\), \(\log2>2/3\); also
\(\log(4/3)<1/3\) and
\(22/7-\pi=\int_0^1x^4(1-x)^4/(1+x^2)\,dx>0\).
Their substitution gives
\(\log((4/3)2^{-13}e^{2\pi})<-43/21<-2\).

The complete exact low-endpoint transfer is now
\[
 \log\mathfrak r_\chi-\log\frac{m_{2q+2l}}{m_{2q}}
 =d_\chi+d_f+t_{H_{\chi,f}}-t_{H_{\chi,0}}
                 -t_{H_{{\rm pow},l}}+t_{H_{{\rm pow},0}}.
 \tag{LET17}
\]
It follows by writing each of the four full integrals as its own bulk
integral times \(e^{t_H}\). In particular, with
\(a_0=E_0+2L_1\) and \(b_0=a_0+\Delta_f\),
\[
 \boxed{
 -a_0\le\log\mathfrak r_\chi-\log\frac{m_{2q+2l}}{m_{2q}}\le b_0.}
 \tag{LET18}
\]
The exact one-sided numerator correction occurs only in the upper bound.
Since
\[
 |a_k|\le|d-g|/(3e),\qquad
 \delta_\chi\le\frac{2(d+g)^2}{3e^3k^2\epsilon^4},\qquad
 \Delta_f\le\frac{1}{16e^2k\epsilon^2},
\]
the finite errors in LET18 are \(O(1)\) at fixed \(m=1,\delta,\gamma\)
and \(O(k^{-1})\) for each fixed \(m\ge2,\delta,\gamma\).
The last displayed bound uses \(S_2\le k^3/16\) and \(q\ge ek^2\).
Both tail corrections have the already proved exponential decay.

\section{The whole original signed Gamma functional}
Retain the full high reference matrices
\[
 (\mathsf M_{a,r})_{ij}=m_{2a+i+j},\qquad
 \mathcal H_{q,l,r}=\det\mathsf M_{q+l,r}/\det\mathsf M_{q,r},
 \qquad r=q,q+1.
\]
Let
\[
 P_k=\log D_{l,q-1}+\log D_{l,q}
       -\log D_{l,2q-1}-\log D_{l,2q},\qquad
 D_{l,N}=\prod_{a=0}^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{2l-1}(a+j+1/2),
\]
\[
 W_k=P_k-\log\frac{m_{2q+2l}}{m_{2q}}
              +\log\mathcal H_{q,l,q}+\log\mathcal H_{q,l,q+1}.
 \tag{LET19}
\]
All factors of the original density are retained in the moment matrices;
CTR53 proves their exact rational moment calculation with mass factors
displayed before cancellation. The exact old identity is
\(\mathfrak T_k=P_k-\log\mathfrak r_\chi+
\log\mathfrak R_{2q-1}+\log\mathfrak R_{2q}\).

Write the proved high-endpoint constants from CTR45--48 as
\[
 A=2E_{\chi,l}+2(L_q+L_{q+1}),\qquad
 B=A+(2q+1)\Delta_f,\qquad E_{\chi,l}=lE_0.
\]
Their complete signed sum has error in \([-A,B]\).
The minus sign of the low endpoint reverses LET18. Therefore
\[
 \boxed{W_k-A-b_0\le\mathfrak T_k\le W_k+B+a_0,}
 \qquad
 \boxed{-W_k-B-a_0\le R_k^0-R_k^\sigma\le -W_k+A+b_0.}
 \tag{LET20}
\]
This is a direct substitution of exact determinant identities and finite
proved errors. In particular the entire Gamma reference \(W_k\) depends
only on the original integers \(q,l\) and the same fixed Gamma density.
The original \(\delta,\gamma\) remain in its error constants, finite
threshold, original quotient, and all exact relation maps.
Both sides have error \(O(k)=o(q)\); at every fixed \(m\ge2\) the error
is \(O(1)\). No value is assigned here to the remaining explicit \(W_k\).

Finally, preserve the exact earlier arithmetic notation
\(\Delta_\Gamma=\delta_\sigma-\mathfrak T_k\) and
\(B_{\rm ar}=B_0+\Delta_\Gamma\), with
\(-a_-\le\delta_\sigma\le a_+\).
Adding these signed intervals gives the completed receiving formula
\[
 B_0-W_k-B-a_0-a_-\ \le B_{\rm ar}\le\
 B_0-W_k+A+b_0+a_+ .
 \tag{LET21}
\]
The baseline \(B_0\) in LET21 is the existing arithmetic quantity,
whereas the lowercase \(a_0,b_0\) are the low-source errors explicitly
defined above. Subtracting the original \(4q\log(D_h k)\) gives the
corresponding interval for the unchanged HC residual.

\section{Original moments and the two sheets of the squared coordinate}

Retain $k=4l+1$, $l\geq1$, $m\geq1$,
$e=1+k(m-1)$, $q=e(k+1)^2$, $c=k/2$, and the original
measure and its mass
\[
 d\sigma(y)=\frac{|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2}{2\pi}\,dy,
 \qquad c_\sigma=\int_{\mathbb R}d\sigma=\sqrt{2\pi}.
\tag{PHT1}
\]
Use distinct names for the ordinary and even moments:
\[
 m_j=\int_{\mathbb R}y^j\,d\sigma(y),\quad
 \mu_j=m_{2j},\quad
 M_{a,r}=(m_{2a+i+j})_{0\leq i,j<r},\quad
 H_n^{(a)}=\det(\mu_{a+i+j})_{0\leq i,j<n}.
\tag{PHT2}
\]
Here $a,n$ are nonnegative integers, $r\geq1$, and
$H_0^{(a)}=1$. All moments are finite: rotation of Euler's
Gamma integral through any angle $0<\theta<\pi/2$ gives
\[
 \sigma(y)\leq\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{2\pi\sqrt{\cos\theta}}
                         e^{-\theta|y|}.
\]
For the rotation use the argument $\operatorname{sgn}(y)\theta$;
the factor contributed by the power is $e^{-\theta|y|/2}$,
and the absolute remaining Euler integral is
$\Gamma(1/4)(\cos\theta)^{-1/4}$ before squaring.
The small and large circular arcs vanish by the positive
real exponent and by $\cos\theta>0$. This proves the displayed
bound and its constants directly.

The positive-axis and negative-axis maps to $t=y^2$ have
inverse branches $y=\sqrt t$ and $y=-\sqrt t$. Each branch
pushes the original measure to
\[
 d\nu_+(t)=d\nu_-(t)=\frac{\sigma(\sqrt t)}{2\sqrt t}\,dt,
 \quad t>0.
\]
The reversed orientation of the negative branch changes the
integration limits as well as its Jacobian, so its measure is
positive with the same factor $1/2$. Thus the full pushforward is
\[
 d\nu(t)=d\nu_+(t)+d\nu_-(t)
        =\frac{\sigma(\sqrt t)}{\sqrt t}\,dt,
 \quad\int_0^\infty d\nu=c_\sigma,
 \quad\mu_j=\int_0^\infty t^j\,d\nu(t).
\tag{PHT3}
\]
No half-axis contribution has been treated as the whole mass.

Every polynomial has a unique decomposition
$P(y)=P_e(y^2)+yP_o(y^2)$. The exact coefficient map
$(P_e,P_o)\mapsto P_e(y^2)+yP_o(y^2)$ has inverse
\[
 P_e(t)=\frac{P(\sqrt t)+P(-\sqrt t)}2,
 \qquad
 P_o(t)=\frac{P(\sqrt t)-P(-\sqrt t)}{2\sqrt t}.
\tag{PHT4}
\]
Adding the two branch integrals cancels their cross terms and gives
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}|P(y)|^2y^{2a}d\sigma(y)
 =\int_0^\infty|P_e(t)|^2t^a d\nu(t)
  +\int_0^\infty|P_o(t)|^2t^{a+1}d\nu(t).
\tag{PHT5}
\]
These same formulas extend to a unitary map of the corresponding
$L^2$ spaces: the inverse in PHT4 is defined for almost every
$t>0$, and PHT5 proves both its range and norm. The point $t=0$
has zero measure. On the exact multiplication domains this map
transports the original real coordinate operator to
\[
 M_y\longleftrightarrow
 \begin{pmatrix}0&M_t\\I&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 M_y^2\longleftrightarrow
 \begin{pmatrix}M_t&0\\0&M_t\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{PHT6}
\]
Indeed multiplication of $P_e(y^2)+yP_o(y^2)$ by $y$ gives
$(tP_o)(y^2)+yP_e(y^2)$. Thus the two parity blocks retain
their exact coupling under the original coordinate.
Explicitly, in the base Hilbert space
\[
 \mathcal K_a=L^2(t^a d\nu)\oplus L^2(t^{a+1}d\nu),
\]
the first operator has domain
\[
 \{(u,v)\in\mathcal K_a:
       u\in L^2(t^{a+1}d\nu),\quad
       v\in L^2(t^{a+2}d\nu)\}
\]
and acts by $(u,v)\mapsto(tv,u)$. Its squared operator has
domain
\[
 \{(u,v)\in\mathcal K_a:
       u\in L^2(t^{a+2}d\nu),\quad
       v\in L^2(t^{a+3}d\nu)\}
\]
and acts by $(u,v)\mapsto(tu,tv)$. These are exactly the
conditions that the displayed images belong to $\mathcal K_a$.
For the squared domain the intermediate domain conditions
follow from $t\leq1+t^2$. PHT5 then identifies these domains
with $\{P:yP\in L^2(y^{2a}d\sigma)\}$ and
$\{P:y^2P\in L^2(y^{2a}d\sigma)\}$, respectively, including
the membership of $P$ in that base space.

\section{The complete finite parity congruence}

Set $n_e=\lceil r/2\rceil$, $n_o=\lfloor r/2\rfloor$.
Let $\Pi_r$ have columns $e_0,e_2,\ldots,e_{2n_e-2}$ followed
by $e_1,e_3,\ldots,e_{2n_o-1}$ in the original coefficient
frame. Then $\Pi_r^{-1}=\Pi_r^{\mathsf T}$ and
$\det\Pi_r=(-1)^{n_e(n_e-1)/2}$: the $j$-th even index crosses
exactly $j$ smaller odd indices. Evenness of $\sigma$ makes
each odd moment zero. Entrywise substitution therefore proves
\[
 \Pi_r^{\mathsf T}M_{a,r}\Pi_r
 =\begin{pmatrix}
  (\mu_{a+i+j})_{i,j<n_e}&0\\
  0&(\mu_{a+1+i+j})_{i,j<n_o}
 \end{pmatrix}.
\tag{PHT7}
\]
This is a congruence in the original coefficient space. Its
two determinant signs cancel, so with $D_{a,r}=\det M_{a,r}$,
\[
 D_{a,2n}=H_n^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)},\qquad
 D_{a,2n+1}=H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)}.
\tag{PHT8}
\]
Every factor is strictly positive: its quadratic form is
the integral of a nonzero polynomial's squared modulus
against a positive density on $(0,\infty)$.

The original $S$ frame is also retained. The map
$P(S)\mapsto P(c+iy)$ has coefficient matrix
\[
 E_{j,n}=\binom nj c^{n-j}(\sqrt{-1})^j\quad(j\leq n),\qquad
 E^{-1}:Q(y)\mapsto Q((S-c)/i),
 \quad\det E=i^{r(r-1)/2}.
\tag{PHT9}
\]
Entries below the displayed triangular range are zero.
The source Gram in the $S$ frame is exactly $E^*M_{a,r}E$,
and its determinant is $D_{a,r}$ because $|\det E|=1$.
Multiplication by $(S-c)^a$ contributes the exact scalar
$i^a$ under this map; its squared modulus is one. These
identities identify the moments in PHT2 with the actual
monomial Gamma relation subspaces, including their degree.

\section{The two original high degrees reduce to one positive shift}

The stipulated $q=e(k+1)^2$ is even; put $n=q/2$. Define
\[
 Z_a=H_n^{(a)}H_{n+1}^{(a)}(H_n^{(a+1)})^2,
 \qquad A_n^{(a)}=H_n^{(a+1)}/H_n^{(a)}>0.
\tag{PHT10}
\]
At the two required ranks $r=q,q+1$, PHT8 proves the exact
combination
\[
 \frac{D_{q+l,q}}{D_{q,q}}
 \frac{D_{q+l,q+1}}{D_{q,q+1}}
 =\frac{Z_{q+l}}{Z_q},\qquad
 \frac{Z_{a+1}}{Z_a}
 =A_n^{(a)}A_{n+1}^{(a)}(A_n^{(a+1)})^2.
\tag{PHT11}
\]
Hence its logarithm is exactly the finite sum
\[
 \log\frac{Z_{q+l}}{Z_q}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}
  \{\log A_n^{(q+j)}+\log A_{n+1}^{(q+j)}
                              +2\log A_n^{(q+j+1)}\}.
\tag{PHT12}
\]
This is a contiguous-shift expression for the original two
high determinants, with their unchanged ranks. The original
mass factor in each $H_n^{(a)}$ is $c_\sigma^n$, so $Z_a$
contains $c_\sigma^{4n+1}=c_\sigma^{2q+1}$. It cancels only
after the numerator and denominator in PHT11 are formed.

\section{Original Toda time and the squared-coordinate time}

For $u\geq0$, define the actual pushed-forward family
\[
 d\nu_{a,u}(t)=t^a e^{-ut}d\nu(t),\qquad
 H_n^{(a)}(u)=\det\left(\int_0^\infty
                    t^{a+i+j}e^{-ut}d\nu(t)\right)_{i,j<n}.
\tag{PHT13}
\]
This is precisely the original $y$-source multiplied by
$e^{-u y^2}$, pushed forward through its two branches.
All moment derivatives at $u=0$ exist from the right by
dominated convergence using the original higher moments.
For $\operatorname{Re}u>0$ they are holomorphic, by the same
domination on compact subsets. The real family with $u<0$
does not have finite mass: Gamma reflection and the Euler
bound give $\sigma(y)\geq C e^{-\pi|y|}$ with
$C=\pi/\Gamma(3/4)^2>0$, and
$e^{|u|t-\pi\sqrt t}$ grows exponentially for sufficiently
large $t$. This proves the actual time domain rather than
assigning an unjustified two-sided strip to the quadratic flow.
The reflection formula here follows on $0<\operatorname{Re}z<1$
by changing $x=tv$ in the Euler double integral, obtaining
$\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\int_0^\infty t^{z-1}/(1+t)\,dt$.
A keyhole contour with argument in $(0,2\pi)$ has residue
$e^{i\pi(z-1)}$ at $-1$ and straight-segment factor
$1-e^{2\pi iz}$; its vanishing circular arcs give the value
$\pi/\sin(\pi z)$. At $z=1/4+iy/2$, take squared moduli
and use $|\Gamma(3/4-iy/2)|\leq\Gamma(3/4)$ from Euler's
integral. Since $|\sin(\pi/4+i\pi y/2)|^2
=\cosh(\pi y)/2$, this proves the stated lower bound.

Let $p_j^{(a,u)}$ be the monic orthogonal polynomials in this
measure, with norms
\[h_j=H_{j+1}^{(a)}(u)/H_j^{(a)}(u).\]
Their coefficients are obtained by inverting the positive
finite moment Gram, so the moment differentiability just
proved also justifies each coefficient derivative below.
Expansion of $tp_j$ and orthogonality give
\[
 tp_j=p_{j+1}+b_jp_j+\alpha_jp_{j-1},\qquad
 \alpha_j=h_j/h_{j-1}>0\ (j\geq1),\quad\alpha_0=0.
\tag{PHT14}
\]
All other lower coefficients vanish because
$\langle tp_j,p_i\rangle=\langle p_j,tp_i\rangle=0$
for $i<j-1$; comparison against $p_{j-1}$ gives $\alpha_j$.
Differentiating orthogonality under the negative tilt proves
$\partial_u p_j=\alpha_jp_{j-1}$, since its coefficient
against every $p_i$ is that of $tp_j$ for $i<j$.
Also $\partial_u h_j=-b_jh_j$. Differentiating
$b_j=h_j^{-1}\int t p_j^2d\nu_{a,u}$ and using PHT14 gives
\[
 \partial_u b_j=\alpha_j-\alpha_{j+1},\qquad
 \partial_u\alpha_j=\alpha_j(b_{j-1}-b_j).
\tag{PHT15}
\]
In detail the derivative of the numerator of $b_j$ is
$2\alpha_jh_j-\|tp_j\|^2
=h_j(\alpha_j-\alpha_{j+1}-b_j^2)$, and differentiating
$h_j^{-1}$ supplies the missing $b_j^2$.
Since $H_n=\prod_{j<n}h_j$, telescoping PHT15 proves
\[
 (\log H_n^{(a)}(u))''
  =\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a)}(u)H_{n-1}^{(a)}(u)}{H_n^{(a)}(u)^2}>0
  \quad(n\geq1),
\tag{PHT16}
\]
or equivalently
$H_nH_n''-(H_n')^2=H_{n+1}H_{n-1}$.
At $u=0$ these derivatives are the proved right derivatives.
Thus the coefficient and sign of the Hankel--Toda equation
are exactly those of the existing original-source Toda calculation.

There is an explicit map to that calculation's original
linear tilt as well. For real $\theta$, on $L^2(y^{2a}d\sigma)$, multiplication
by $e^{\theta y/2}$ is the isometry from the source weighted
by $e^{\theta y}$ to the original source, with inverse
given by the negative exponential. Under PHT4--5 the same
map is the following exact operator matrix on the two branches:
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \cosh(\theta\sqrt t/2)&\sqrt t\sinh(\theta\sqrt t/2)\\
  t^{-1/2}\sinh(\theta\sqrt t/2)&\cosh(\theta\sqrt t/2)
 \end{pmatrix}.
\tag{PHT17}
\]
Substituting $y=\sqrt t$ and $y=-\sqrt t$ proves each entry
and its inverse at $-\theta$. To specify the weighted source
and its full cross Gram, write this matrix as $B_{\theta/2}(t)$.
The two branch integrals give the pulled-back form
\[
 \int_0^\infty
 \begin{pmatrix}\overline u&\overline v\end{pmatrix}
 G_\theta(t)\binom uv\,t^a d\nu(t),\qquad
 G_\theta(t)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \cosh(\theta\sqrt t)&\sqrt t\sinh(\theta\sqrt t)\\
  \sqrt t\sinh(\theta\sqrt t)&t\cosh(\theta\sqrt t)
 \end{pmatrix}.
\tag{PHT17a}
\]
For real $\theta$, direct matrix multiplication, using
$\cosh^2 x+\sinh^2 x=\cosh(2x)$ and
$2\sinh x\cosh x=\sinh(2x)$, proves
\[
 B_{\theta/2}(t)^*\operatorname{diag}(1,t)B_{\theta/2}(t)
       =G_\theta(t),\qquad
 B_{-\theta/2}(t)B_{\theta/2}(t)=I.
\]
Thus the space of measurable pairs with finite PHT17a norm
is mapped isometrically and onto $\mathcal K_a$ by
$B_{\theta/2}$, with inverse $B_{-\theta/2}$; the equality of
forms proves the domain and range even when these matrices
are unbounded on the base space. If viewed as an operator
on $\mathcal K_a$ itself, its domain consists precisely of
the pairs in $\mathcal K_a$ whose PHT17a integral is finite.
The branch map in PHT4 identifies that source with the full
original space $L^2(e^{\theta y}y^{2a}d\sigma)$.
The quadratic tilt in PHT13
instead gives the diagonal multiplication $e^{-ut/2}$ in
both components. Both are the specified functions of the
operator in PHT6. This proves the relation between the two
times on the original spaces and keeps the parity mixing of
the original linear time.

For real $|\theta|<\pi/2$ the tilted $y$-moments and their
derivatives converge: in the earlier Gamma bound choose
$|\theta|<\theta_0<\pi/2$. The same orthogonal-polynomial
derivative calculation, with real variable $y$ and positive
tilt $e^{\theta y}$, applies to the exactly defined matrix
$M_{a,r}(\theta)_{ij}=\int_{\mathbb R}y^{2a+i+j}
e^{\theta y}d\sigma(y)$ and proves
\[
 (\log\det M_{a,r}(\theta))''\big|_{\theta=0}
   =h_r^{y,a}/h_{r-1}^{y,a}.
\tag{PHT18}
\]
Here the superscript $y,a$ denotes the full original real-line
monic norms. Parity and PHT5 identify them exactly as
$h_{2j}^{y,a}=H_{j+1}^{(a)}/H_j^{(a)}$ and
$h_{2j+1}^{y,a}=H_{j+1}^{(a+1)}/H_j^{(a+1)}$.
Consequently the two original Toda curvatures have the explicit
positive expressions
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{h_{2n}^{y,a}}{h_{2n-1}^{y,a}}
 &=\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_{n-1}^{(a+1)}}
        {H_n^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)}},\\
 \frac{h_{2n+1}^{y,a}}{h_{2n}^{y,a}}
 &=\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a+1)}H_n^{(a)}}
        {H_n^{(a+1)}H_{n+1}^{(a)}}.
\end{aligned}\tag{PHT19}
\]
These are exact specializations of the original norm-ratio
Toda controls at the ranks needed by the programme. The
contiguous identities and their positive computation follow
in the attached complete derivation.

% Complete independent derivation. Source weight and moment scale are retained.
% This is an input fragment; no build or publication action is asserted.
\section{Exact contiguous Gamma determinants and positive Christoffel evolution}
\label{sec:HCT}

\paragraph{HCT1. Original measure and the typed square map.}
Throughout this section
\[
 d\sigma(y)=\frac{|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2}{2\pi}\,dy,
 \qquad \gamma_0=\sqrt{2\pi},\qquad y\in\mathbb R.
\]
Let $s:\mathbb R\longrightarrow[0,\infty)$ be $s(y)=y^2$ and
let $\nu=s_*\sigma$. For every nonnegative Borel function $g$,
change of variable on each half of the original real line gives
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT1}
 \int_{\mathbb R}g(y^2)\,d\sigma(y)
 =\int_0^\infty g(t)\,
 \frac{|\Gamma(1/4+i\sqrt t/2)|^2}{2\pi\sqrt t}\,dt.
\end{equation}
Thus the factor $t^{-1/2}$ includes both preimages of $t>0$.
For integers $a,n\geq0$ put
\[
 d\nu_a(t)=t^a\,d\nu(t),\qquad
 \mu_n=\int_{\mathbb R}y^{2n}\,d\sigma(y)=\int_0^\infty t^n\,d\nu(t),
 \qquad H_n^{(a)}=\det[\mu_{a+i+j}]_{i,j=0}^{n-1},\quad H_0^{(a)}=1.
\]
No factor $\gamma_0$ is removed from any $H_n^{(a)}$.

All moments exist. Indeed, rotation by $\theta\operatorname{sgn}(y)$
in Euler's Gamma integral, with $0<\theta<\pi/2$, gives
\[
 |\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|
 \leq \Gamma(1/4)(\cos\theta)^{-1/4}e^{-\theta|y|/2}.
\]
The rotation is justified on a truncated sector: the small circular
arc is $O(\epsilon^{1/4})$, and the large arc tends to zero because
$\cos\theta>0$. Squaring proves exponential decay of $\sigma$.
The Gamma function is continuous and nonzero at $1/4$, so the density
is positive on an interval about zero. Every nonzero polynomial has
positive squared integral against every $\nu_a$; consequently
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT2}
 H_n^{(a)}>0\qquad(a\geq0, n\geq0).
\end{equation}

\paragraph{HCT2. Gamma mass, moments, and the full real-line polynomial norms.}
The constants needed below can be derived directly from the original
Gamma weight. For $\lambda=1/4$ set $g(x)=e^{\lambda x-e^x}$.
With the Fourier convention
$\widehat g(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R}g(x)e^{i\xi x}\,dx$, Euler's
integral gives $\widehat g(\xi)=\Gamma(\lambda+i\xi)$.
The function $g$ and all its derivatives decay faster than every
inverse power at both ends. Fourier inversion of its autocorrelation
therefore gives
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}e^{iu\xi}|\Gamma(\lambda+i\xi)|^2\,d\xi
 =2\pi\int_{\mathbb R}g(x+u/2)g(x-u/2)\,dx
 =\frac{2\pi\Gamma(2\lambda)}{(2\cosh(u/2))^{2\lambda}}.
\]
The last equality follows by $v=e^x$ in the integral on the right.
Substituting $y=2\xi$ and $u=2w$ gives the exact characteristic
function
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}e^{iwy}\,d\sigma(y)
 =\gamma_0(\cosh w)^{-1/2}\qquad(w\in\mathbb R).
\]
The exponential bound in HCT1 permits its analytic continuation
to the open strip $|\operatorname{Im}w|<\pi/2$. In particular
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT3}
 \int_{\mathbb R}e^{zy}\,d\sigma(y)
 =\gamma_0(\cos z)^{-1/2}\quad(|z|<\pi/2),\qquad
 \mu_n=\gamma_0(2n)![z^{2n}](\cos z)^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
The square root in (HCT3) is positive at $z=0$.

Define monic polynomials $p_j(y)$ by the convergent generating function
on $|z|<1$, with the branches analytic at zero and equal to their
real values there,
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT4}
 \sum_{j=0}^\infty p_j(y)\frac{z^j}{j!}
 =(1+z^2)^{-1/4}\exp(y\arctan z).
\end{equation}
For sufficiently small real $z,w$, (HCT3) and
\[
 \cos(\arctan z+\arctan w)
 =\frac{1-zw}{\sqrt{1+z^2}\sqrt{1+w^2}}
\]
show that the integral of the product of the two generating functions
is $\gamma_0(1-zw)^{-1/2}$. Derivatives may pass under the integral
by the exponential bound in HCT1. Comparison of coefficients proves
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT5}
 \int_{\mathbb R}p_i(y)p_j(y)\,d\sigma(y)
 =\mathbf1_{i=j}\,\gamma_0\,j!(1/2)_j.
\end{equation}
Here $(b)_j=b(b+1)\cdots(b+j-1)$ and $(b)_0=1$.
Differentiating (HCT4) gives, with $p_{-1}=0$,
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT6}
 y p_j(y)=p_{j+1}(y)+j(j-1/2)p_{j-1}(y),\qquad p_0=1.
\end{equation}
In particular $p_{2n}$ is even and $p_{2n+1}$ is odd. This also
follows directly from $p_j(-y)=(-1)^j p_j(y)$ in (HCT4).

\paragraph{HCT3. Monic polynomials on the actual square-pushforward.}
Let $P_n^{(a)}(t)$ be the monic polynomial orthogonal to
$1,t,\ldots,t^{n-1}$ in $L^2(\nu_a)$, and write
$h_n^{(a)}=\int(P_n^{(a)})^2\,d\nu_a$.
Positivity (HCT2) gives existence and uniqueness by finite
Gram elimination. The determinant formula and its constant term are
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT7}
 P_n^{(a)}(t)=\frac1{H_n^{(a)}}
 \det\begin{bmatrix}
 \mu_a&\mu_{a+1}&\cdots&\mu_{a+n}\\
 \mu_{a+1}&\mu_{a+2}&\cdots&\mu_{a+n+1}\\
 \vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
 \mu_{a+n-1}&\mu_{a+n}&\cdots&\mu_{a+2n-1}\\
 1&t&\cdots&t^n
 \end{bmatrix},\qquad
 P_n^{(a)}(0)=(-1)^n\frac{H_n^{(a+1)}}{H_n^{(a)}}.
\end{equation}
For $n=0$ this means $P_0^{(a)}=1$. The last-row expansion in the
first column proves the displayed sign $(-1)^n$. Orthogonality
follows by integrating against $t^j$, which duplicates a row for
$0\leq j<n$. A monic triangular change from monomials to the
$P_j^{(a)}$ has determinant one, so
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT8}
 H_n^{(a)}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}h_j^{(a)},\qquad
 h_n^{(a)}=\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a)}}{H_n^{(a)}}>0.
\end{equation}

Define
\begin{align}
 c_n^{(a)}&=\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a+1)}H_n^{(a)}}
 {H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)}}>0 \quad(n\geq0),\tag{HCT9}\\
 d_n^{(a)}&=\frac{H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_{n-1}^{(a+1)}}
 {H_n^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)}}>0 \quad(n\geq1),\qquad d_0^{(a)}=0.\tag{HCT10}
\end{align}
The exact Christoffel and inverse connection identities are
\begin{align}
 tP_n^{(a+1)}(t)&=P_{n+1}^{(a)}(t)+c_n^{(a)}P_n^{(a)}(t),\tag{HCT11}\\
 P_n^{(a)}(t)&=P_n^{(a+1)}(t)+d_n^{(a)}P_{n-1}^{(a+1)}(t)
 \quad(n\geq1).\tag{HCT12}
\end{align}
For $n=0$ the second identity reads $1=1$.
To prove (HCT11), (HCT7) gives
$P_{n+1}^{(a)}(0)+c_n^{(a)}P_n^{(a)}(0)=0$.
Division by $t$ is consequently polynomial division with zero
remainder. The quotient is monic of degree $n$ and has zero
$\nu_{a+1}$ inner product with $t^j$ for $j<n$; uniqueness proves
the identity. Taking its $\nu_a$ inner product with
$P_n^{(a+1)}$ proves
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT13}
 h_n^{(a+1)}=c_n^{(a)}h_n^{(a)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the $P_{n+1}^{(a)}$ term has zero inner product, while
$P_n^{(a+1)}-P_n^{(a)}$ has degree at most $n-1$.
For (HCT12), $P_n^{(a)}-P_n^{(a+1)}$ has degree at most $n-1$.
Its $\nu_{a+1}$ inner products with all polynomials of degree at
most $n-2$ vanish, because multiplication of such a polynomial by
$t$ has degree at most $n-1$. It is therefore a multiple of
$P_{n-1}^{(a+1)}$. The numerator of that coefficient is
\[
 \int P_n^{(a)}P_{n-1}^{(a+1)}\,d\nu_{a+1}
 =\int P_n^{(a)}\,tP_{n-1}^{(a+1)}\,d\nu_a=h_n^{(a)},
\]
using (HCT11) at $n-1$. Dividing by $h_{n-1}^{(a+1)}$ gives
exactly (HCT10), including its positive sign.

\paragraph{HCT4. Positive discrete Toda identities.}
Combining (HCT11) and (HCT12) twice, entirely within the original
polynomial spaces, gives
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT14}
 tP_n^{(a)}=P_{n+1}^{(a)}+
 (c_n^{(a)}+d_n^{(a)})P_n^{(a)}
 +d_n^{(a)}c_{n-1}^{(a)}P_{n-1}^{(a)}.
\end{equation}
At $n=0$ the last term is absent. Expanding instead in the
$a+1$ basis gives
\[
 tP_n^{(a+1)}=P_{n+1}^{(a+1)}+
 (c_n^{(a)}+d_{n+1}^{(a)})P_n^{(a+1)}
 +c_n^{(a)}d_n^{(a)}P_{n-1}^{(a+1)}.
\]
Coefficient comparison with (HCT14) at $a+1$ proves
\begin{align}
 c_n^{(a+1)}+d_n^{(a+1)}
 &=c_n^{(a)}+d_{n+1}^{(a)} &&(n\geq0),\tag{HCT15}\\
 c_{n-1}^{(a+1)}d_n^{(a+1)}
 &=c_n^{(a)}d_n^{(a)} &&(n\geq1).\tag{HCT16}
\end{align}
All nonzero factors in these identities are positive. In the
notation $b_n^{(a)}=c_n^{(a)}+d_n^{(a)}$ and
$u_n^{(a)}=d_n^{(a)}c_{n-1}^{(a)}$, the two recurrences give the
complete $b,u$ coefficients and their shifted values, including
$b_n^{(a+1)}=c_n^{(a)}+d_{n+1}^{(a)}$ and
$u_n^{(a+1)}=c_n^{(a)}d_n^{(a)}$.

Here is the exact polynomial meaning of the matrix factorization.
Use column vectors
\[\mathbf P^{(a)}=(P_0^{(a)},P_1^{(a)},\ldots)^T\]
as formal polynomial sequences. Let $L_a$ have diagonal $1$ and
subdiagonal $(L_a)_{n,n-1}=d_n^{(a)}$, and let $U_a$ have diagonal
$c_n^{(a)}$ and superdiagonal $1$. Every row has finite support.
Then (HCT11)--(HCT12) state
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT17}
 \mathbf P^{(a)}=L_a\mathbf P^{(a+1)},\qquad
 t\mathbf P^{(a+1)}=U_a\mathbf P^{(a)},\qquad
 J_a=L_aU_a,\quad J_{a+1}=U_aL_a.
\end{equation}
Consequently $J_{a+1}U_a=U_aJ_a$ and $J_aL_a=L_aJ_{a+1}$.
These are row-finite polynomial identities; no infinite-dimensional
bounded-operator assertion or truncation of an endpoint row is
needed. At a finite degree the identities retain every displayed
$P_{n+1}$ term. The scalar multiplication map is literally
$t:\mathbb R[t]_{\leq n}\to\mathbb R[t]_{\leq n+1}$.

\paragraph{HCT5. Exact initial Gamma data and a subtraction-free finite evolution.}
Parity, (HCT1), and uniqueness give the exact source identifications
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT18}
 p_{2n}(y)=P_n^{(0)}(y^2),\qquad
 p_{2n+1}(y)=yP_n^{(1)}(y^2).
\end{equation}
Thus (HCT5) gives, with the original mass retained,
\begin{align}
 h_n^{(0)}&=\gamma_0(2n)!(1/2)_{2n},&
 H_n^{(0)}&=\gamma_0^n\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(2j)!(1/2)_{2j},\tag{HCT19}\\
 h_n^{(1)}&=\gamma_0(2n+1)!(1/2)_{2n+1},&
 H_n^{(1)}&=\gamma_0^n\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(2j+1)!(1/2)_{2j+1}.\tag{HCT20}
\end{align}
Equations (HCT9)--(HCT10) therefore yield the exact positive
rational starting values
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT21}
 c_n^{(0)}=(2n+1)(2n+1/2),\qquad
 d_n^{(0)}=2n(2n-1/2),\qquad n\geq0.
\end{equation}

For a fixed step $a\to a+1$ introduce auxiliary numbers $e_n$ by
\begin{align}
 e_0&=c_0^{(a)},&d_0^{(a+1)}&=0,&
 c_0^{(a+1)}&=e_0+d_1^{(a)},\tag{HCT22}\\
 e_n&=\frac{c_n^{(a)}e_{n-1}}{e_{n-1}+d_n^{(a)}},&
 d_n^{(a+1)}&=\frac{c_n^{(a)}d_n^{(a)}}{e_{n-1}+d_n^{(a)}},&
 c_n^{(a+1)}&=e_n+d_{n+1}^{(a)}\quad(n\geq1).\tag{HCT23}
\end{align}
Every denominator and every $e_n,c_n^{(a+1)}$ is strictly positive;
$d_n^{(a+1)}>0$ for $n\geq1$. These formulas use only positive
sums, products, and quotients. To verify them, (HCT15) at zero
gives (HCT22). If $c_{n-1}^{(a+1)}=e_{n-1}+d_n^{(a)}$, then
(HCT16) gives the middle formula in (HCT23). The first formula
implies $e_n+d_n^{(a+1)}=c_n^{(a)}$; substituting this in
(HCT15) gives the last formula. This proves the recurrence and
the claimed positivity by induction.

It follows, without determinant cancellation, that for any finite
integer $a,n\geq0$
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT24}
 H_n^{(a)}=H_n^{(0)}
 \prod_{s=0}^{a-1}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(s)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $H_n^{(a+1)}/H_n^{(a)}=\prod_{j<n}c_j^{(a)}$ follows
from (HCT8) and (HCT13), and then telescopes in $a$.
An initial array through index $N+A$ suffices to compute all
$c_n^{(a)},d_n^{(a)}$ with $a\leq A$, $n\leq N$: one may
retain indices $0\leq n\leq N+A-a$ after step $a$ and shrink
the retained interval by one at each subsequent step.
Thus the formulas give a finite exact calculation for arbitrarily
specified original ranks and shifts. The assertion here concerns
an exact finite recurrence, with no unproved assertion about its
computational cost or large-rank limit.

\paragraph{HCT6. Sign-correct contiguous determinants.}
For $n\geq1$, apply the Desnanot--Jacobi identity to the
$(n+1)\times(n+1)$ matrix $[\mu_{a+i+j}]_{i,j=0}^{n}$,
deleting its first and last rows and columns. The two principal
minors are $H_n^{(a+2)}$ and $H_n^{(a)}$, the two cross minors
are both $H_n^{(a+1)}$, and the central minor is
$H_{n-1}^{(a+2)}$. Their original index order is retained. Hence
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT25}
 H_n^{(a)}H_n^{(a+2)}-(H_n^{(a+1)})^2
 =H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_{n-1}^{(a+2)}>0.
\end{equation}
For completeness, the determinant identity used here follows by
Schur-complement elimination of the central block, whose determinant
is positive by (HCT2). The remaining $2\times2$ determinant is
the product of its diagonal entries minus the product of its
off-diagonal entries; multiplication by the central determinant
squared gives (HCT25). For $n=1$ the central block has size zero
and determinant one, giving the same identity directly.

Put $\kappa_0^{(a)}=0$ and, for $n\geq1$,
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT26}
 \kappa_n^{(a)}=
 \frac{H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_{n-1}^{(a+2)}}{(H_n^{(a+1)})^2}>0.
\end{equation}
Then the exact logarithmic second difference is
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT27}
 \log H_n^{(a+2)}-2\log H_n^{(a+1)}+\log H_n^{(a)}
 =\log(1+\kappa_n^{(a)}).
\end{equation}
For $n\geq1$ its equivalent positive Toda expression is
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT28}
 0<\frac{d_n^{(a)}}{c_{n-1}^{(a+1)}}
 =\frac{\kappa_n^{(a)}}{1+\kappa_n^{(a)}}<1,\qquad
 \log(1+\kappa_n^{(a)})
 =-\log\left(1-\frac{d_n^{(a)}}{c_{n-1}^{(a+1)}}\right).
\end{equation}
Substitution of (HCT9)--(HCT10), followed by (HCT25), proves
every equality and inequality in (HCT28).
In particular, if $R_n^{(a)}=H_n^{(a+1)}/H_n^{(a)}$, then
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT29}
 R_n^{(a)}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(a)},\qquad
 \frac{R_n^{(a+1)}}{R_n^{(a)}}=1+\kappa_n^{(a)}.
\end{equation}
For a finite integer $l\geq1$, exact telescoping gives
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT30}
 \log\frac{H_n^{(a+l+1)}/H_n^{(a+1)}}
 {H_n^{(a+l)}/H_n^{(a)}}
 =\sum_{s=0}^{l-1}\log(1+\kappa_n^{(a+s)}).
\end{equation}
This quantity is strictly positive when $n\geq1$.
The signed claim in (HCT27)--(HCT30) concerns $a$-differences
of the indicated determinant ratios. The degree difference is
the different exact identity
$\log R_{n+1}^{(a)}-\log R_n^{(a)}=\log c_n^{(a)}$;
no sign or asymptotic size of this last logarithm is needed.

\paragraph{HCT7. Return to both original full matrices.}
Retain the full matrices from the original real coordinate:
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT31}
 M_{a,r}[i,j]=\int_{\mathbb R}y^{2a+i+j}\,d\sigma(y)
 \quad(0\leq i,j<r),\qquad \mathcal D_{a,r}=\det M_{a,r}.
\end{equation}
Let $n_0=\lceil r/2\rceil$, $n_1=\lfloor r/2\rfloor$.
Permute the original indices to $0,2,4,\ldots,1,3,5,\ldots$
using the same permutation in the source and its dual. Odd
moments vanish by evenness of $\sigma$. The even-even block is
$[\mu_{a+i+j}]_{i,j<n_0}$ and the odd-odd block is
$[\mu_{a+1+i+j}]_{i,j<n_1}$. Thus the exact congruence and
its determinant are
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT32}
 P_r M_{a,r}P_r^T=
 \operatorname{diag}\big([\mu_{a+i+j}]_{i,j<n_0},
 [\mu_{a+1+i+j}]_{i,j<n_1}\big),\qquad
 \mathcal D_{a,r}=H_{n_0}^{(a)}H_{n_1}^{(a+1)}.
\end{equation}
The determinant sign is $\det(P_r)^2=1$.
At the function-space level the same map sends the coefficient
vector of $p(t)+yq(t)$, with $t=y^2$, to the two coefficient
vectors of $(p,q)$. Its exact norm identity is
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}y^{2a}|p(y^2)+yq(y^2)|^2\,d\sigma(y)
 =\int_0^\infty|p(t)|^2\,d\nu_a(t)
  +\int_0^\infty|q(t)|^2\,d\nu_{a+1}(t).
\]
The cross term is odd. This proves the typed orthogonal direct-sum
map with both original parity sectors retained.

For the even integer $q=e(k+1)^2=2n$ specified by the packet,
the two original ranks are therefore exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT33}
 \mathcal D_{a,q}=H_n^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)},\qquad
 \mathcal D_{a,q+1}=H_{n+1}^{(a)}H_n^{(a+1)}.
\end{equation}
Only the stipulated integrality and evenness of $q$ are used here;
the packet parameter $e$ is left unchanged.
In particular
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT34}
 \frac{\mathcal D_{a,q+1}}{\mathcal D_{a,q}}=h_n^{(a)},\qquad
 \frac{\mathcal D_{a+l,q+1}/\mathcal D_{a,q+1}}
 {\mathcal D_{a+l,q}/\mathcal D_{a,q}}
 =\prod_{s=0}^{l-1}c_n^{(a+s)}.
\end{equation}
The empty product at $l=0$ is one.

Write $Z_a=\mathcal D_{a,q}\mathcal D_{a,q+1}$ while keeping
the two original ranks fixed. Equations (HCT29), (HCT33) give
\begin{align}
 Z_a&=H_n^{(a)}H_{n+1}^{(a)}(H_n^{(a+1)})^2,\tag{HCT35}\\
 \frac{Z_{a+1}}{Z_a}
 &=R_n^{(a)}R_{n+1}^{(a)}(R_n^{(a+1)})^2,\tag{HCT36}\\
 \frac{Z_{q+l}}{Z_q}
 &=\prod_{s=0}^{l-1}
 \left\{
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s)}\right)
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n}c_j^{(q+s)}\right)
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s+1)}\right)^2
 \right\}.\tag{HCT37}
\end{align}
Equation (HCT37) evaluates the paired determinant ratio at the
requested original exponents $a=q,q+l$ by the positive rational
evolution (HCT21)--(HCT23). Each of the four factors supplied by
the two full matrices remains visible. The $\gamma_0$ factors
in (HCT19)--(HCT24) cancel in this particular ratio because both
numerator and denominator have the same total matrix dimension;
they are present in the individual determinants throughout.

Finally the exact curvature of this same paired determinant is
\begin{equation}\tag{HCT38}
 \frac{Z_{a+2}Z_a}{Z_{a+1}^2}
 =(1+\kappa_n^{(a)})(1+\kappa_{n+1}^{(a)})
  (1+\kappa_n^{(a+1)})^2>1.
\end{equation}
For $l\geq1$ the consequent finite shift identity is
\begin{align}
 \log\frac{Z_{a+l+1}/Z_{a+1}}{Z_{a+l}/Z_a}
 =\sum_{s=0}^{l-1}\big[&\log(1+\kappa_n^{(a+s)})
 +\log(1+\kappa_{n+1}^{(a+s)})\notag\\*
 &+2\log(1+\kappa_n^{(a+s+1)})\big]>0.\tag{HCT39}
\end{align}
These statements follow by adding (HCT27) for exactly the four
blocks in (HCT35), exponentiating for (HCT38), and telescoping
for (HCT39); they make no replacement of $q$ by an
independent large parameter. They provide the full finite return,
its positive contiguous increments, and its exact degree-step
comparison. A growth rate along the packet values of $q,k,l$
requires evaluating these explicit positive quantities; no such
rate is assumed in any identity above.

% END INLINE SOURCE 2


\section{The complete universal Gamma center is a positive rational product}

Use exactly the all-index moments $m_j$ in PHT2. The universal
center identified in the low-endpoint transport calculation is
the following finite quantity:
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \mathscr D_{l,N}=\prod_{a=0}^N\prod_{j=0}^{2l-1}(a+j+1/2),\\
 P_k=\log\mathscr D_{l,q-1}+\log\mathscr D_{l,q}
             -\log\mathscr D_{l,2q-1}-\log\mathscr D_{l,2q},\\
 W_k=P_k-\log\frac{m_{2q+2l}}{m_{2q}}
     +\log\frac{D_{q+l,q}}{D_{q,q}}
     +\log\frac{D_{q+l,q+1}}{D_{q,q+1}}.
\end{gathered}\tag{PHT20}
\]
Here $\mathscr D_{l,N}$ denotes exactly the source product
called $D_{l,N}$ in GCR and LET, while $D_{a,r}$ retains
its moment-determinant definition from PHT8. Define
\[
 G_{q,l}=
 \left(\prod_{a=q}^{2q-1}\prod_{j=0}^{2l-1}(a+j+1/2)\right)
 \left(\prod_{a=q+1}^{2q}\prod_{j=0}^{2l-1}(a+j+1/2)\right)>0.
\tag{PHT21}
\]
Cancellation of the explicitly overlapping monic products
gives $e^{P_k}=G_{q,l}^{-1}$. The low moment ratio retains
its precise index conversion:
\[
 \frac{m_{2q+2l}}{m_{2q}}
 =\frac{\mu_{q+l}}{\mu_q}
 =\frac{H_1^{(q+l)}}{H_1^{(q)}}
 =\prod_{s=0}^{l-1}c_0^{(q+s)}.
\tag{PHT22}
\]
The last equality is HCT13 at degree zero and then finite
telescoping. Combining PHT11, HCT37 and PHT22 proves
\[
\begin{gathered}
 W_k=\log\mathcal W_{q,l},\\
 \boxed{\mathcal W_{q,l}=
 \frac1{G_{q,l}}\prod_{s=0}^{l-1}
 \frac{\displaystyle
   \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s)}\right)
   \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n}c_j^{(q+s)}\right)
   \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s+1)}\right)^2}
 {c_0^{(q+s)}},\qquad n=q/2.}
\end{gathered}\tag{PHT23}
\]
Every factor is positive and rational: the initial $c,d$
values are the exact rational Gamma values HCT21, and
HCT22--23 use only positive sums, products and quotients.
The initial norms still contain $c_\sigma$, as HCT19--20
record. Their common powers cancel in PHT11 and PHT22
only after their full source determinants are constructed.

Thus PHT23 is a finite evaluation procedure for the entire
universal center in PHT20, requiring neither a new moment
quadrature nor a difference of nearly equal Gram determinants.
One sufficient finite initial range is $0\leq j\leq n+q+l$:
HCT22--23 then compute all needed coefficients through shift
$q+l$ with the proved decreasing-index range in HCT24.
This statement specifies an exact finite procedure. It does
not assert a bit-complexity estimate or an asymptotic value
along the original coupled sequence $q=e(k+1)^2$, $l=(k-1)/4$.
The positive contiguous identities HCT25--39 provide the
exact equations for that next calculation, with all ranks
and original source factors retained.

\section{The original four-product cancellation with a finite remainder}
\label{sec:WGP}

Retain $k=4l+1$, $l\ge1$, $m\ge1$, $e=1+k(m-1)$ and
$q=e(k+1)^2=2n$. The symbols $e$ and $n$ here are the original
packet multiplicity and the exact half of its degree. Define the original
source products and their signed combination by
\[
 D_{l,N}=\prod_{a=0}^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{2l-1}(a+j+1/2),\qquad
 P_k=\log D_{l,q-1}+\log D_{l,q}
       -\log D_{l,2q-1}-\log D_{l,2q}.
 \tag{WGP1}
\]
The present calculation estimates these literal products, including both
distinct endpoint ranges. In particular it does not replace the two ranges
by a common interval and omit their endpoint correction.

Cancelling identical factors in the four specified finite products gives
\[
 P_k=-\sum_{j=0}^{2l-1}\left\{
 \sum_{a=q}^{2q-1}\log(a+j+1/2)
 +\sum_{a=q+1}^{2q}\log(a+j+1/2)\right\}.
 \tag{WGP2}
\]
Put $x_b=(b+1/2)/q$ for $q\le b\le2q-1$. The change of index
$b=a-1$ in the second sum retains its full endpoints and yields
\[
 \widetilde P_{q,l}:=P_k+4lq\log q
 =-\sum_{b=q}^{2q-1}\sum_{j=0}^{2l-1}
  \left\{\log(x_b+j/q)+\log(x_b+(j+1)/q)\right\}.
 \tag{WGP3}
\]
This defines an explicitly recorded scalar correction to $P_k$; the
original products in WGP1 are still the objects to which it refers.

\paragraph{An elementary quadrature bound with its sign.}
For a twice continuously differentiable real $F$ on $[1,2]$, divide
that interval into the $q$ cells of width $h=1/q$ with centres $x_b$.
The identity
\[
 F(x_b+u)-F(x_b)-uF'(x_b)
 =\begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\int_0^u(u-v)F''(x_b+v)\,dv,&u\ge0,\\
 \displaystyle\int_u^0(v-u)F''(x_b+v)\,dv,&u<0
 \end{cases}
\]
follows by two integrations of $F''$. Its absolute value is at most
$\|F''\|_\infty u^2/2$. Integration over each symmetric cell kills the
linear term, giving
\[
 \left|\sum_b F(x_b)-q\int_1^2F(x)\,dx\right|
 \le\frac{\|F''\|_\infty}{24q}.
 \tag{WGP4}
\]
The remainder has the sign of $F''$ when this sign is constant; hence
the midpoint sum is below the integral multiplied by $q$ for convex $F$
and above it for concave $F$. Applying the formula to the three literal
functions $\log x,x^{-1},x^{-2}$ gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_b\log x_b&=q(2\log2-1)+\varepsilon_0,
       &0\le\varepsilon_0\le(24q)^{-1},\\
 \sum_b x_b^{-1}&=q\log2-\varepsilon_1,
       &0\le\varepsilon_1\le(12q)^{-1},\\
 \sum_b x_b^{-2}&=q/2-\varepsilon_2,
       &0\le\varepsilon_2\le(4q)^{-1}.
 \end{aligned}\tag{WGP5}
\]
Here the second derivative absolute bounds are respectively $1,2,6$.
The integrals are evaluated by their displayed elementary antiderivatives.

For every $u\ge0$, integrating
$1/(1+u)=1-u+u^2/(1+u)$ gives
\[
 \log(1+u)=u-u^2/2+r(u),\qquad
 r(u)=\int_0^u\frac{t^2}{1+t}\,dt,\qquad 0\le r(u)\le u^3/3.
 \tag{WGP6}
\]
Use this exact formula at $u=j/(qx_b)$ and $(j+1)/(qx_b)$.
The finite sums of their numerators are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j<2l}(j+j+1)&=4l^2,\\
 \sum_{j<2l}(j^2+(j+1)^2)&=(16l^3+2l)/3,\\
 \sum_{j<2l}(j^3+(j+1)^3)&=8l^4+2l^2.
 \end{aligned}\tag{WGP7}
\]
These identities follow by adding the two shifted ranges; they also follow
directly from telescoping the differences of the powers $j^2,j^3,j^4$.
Consequently WGP3 is the exact identity
\[
 \widetilde P_{q,l}
 =-4l\sum_b\log x_b-\frac{4l^2}{q}\sum_bx_b^{-1}
   +\frac{8l^3+l}{3q^2}\sum_bx_b^{-2}-\mathcal R_{q,l},
 \tag{WGP8}
\]
where the original sum of the $2(2l)q$ nonnegative remainders satisfies
\[
 0\le\mathcal R_{q,l}
 \le\frac{8l^4+2l^2}{3q^3}\sum_bx_b^{-3}
 \le\frac{8l^4+2l^2}{3q^2}.
 \tag{WGP9}
\]
The last inequality uses $x_b\ge1$ and the exact number $q$ of cells.
There is no smallness hypothesis on $j/q$ in WGP6--9.

Substituting WGP5 gives the finite expansion
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 P_k={}&-4lq\log q-4lq(2\log2-1)-4l^2\log2
             +\frac{8l^3+l}{6q}+\mathcal E_{q,l},\\
 -\mathcal E^-_{q,l}\le{}&\mathcal E_{q,l}\le\mathcal E^+_{q,l},\\
 \mathcal E^-_{q,l}={}&\frac{l}{6q}
       +\frac{8l^3+l}{12q^3}
       +\frac{8l^4+2l^2}{3q^2},\qquad
 \mathcal E^+_{q,l}=\frac{l^2}{3q^2}.
 \end{aligned}}\tag{WGP10}
\]
Indeed the exact error is
$-4l\varepsilon_0+(4l^2/q)\varepsilon_1
 -(8l^3+l)\varepsilon_2/(3q^2)-\mathcal R_{q,l}$.
Every coefficient is positive before its displayed sign is applied;
the inequalities in WGP10 follow separately from WGP5 and WGP9.
Since $q\ge(4l+2)^2$, this error is $O(1)$ at fixed $m=1$.
For any fixed $m\ge2$ it is $O(l^{-2})$ as $l\to\infty$, because
$e=1+(4l+1)(m-1)$ and hence $q$ is of order $l^3$.
In particular the explicit $-4l^2\log2$ term survives on the $q$ scale
at $m=1$ and has been retained. The next term is also displayed with
its sign and original endpoints rather than absorbed into that scale.

\paragraph{Exact interface to the positive original Gamma recurrence.}
The Gamma measure is still
$d\sigma(y)=|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2dy/(2\pi)$.
For $a,j\ge0$ let $H_j^{(a)}$ and $c_j^{(a)}$ have exactly their
HCT definitions: $H_j^{(a)}=\det[\int y^{2(a+i+r)}d\sigma]_{i,r<j}$
and $c_j^{(a)}=H_{j+1}^{(a+1)}H_j^{(a)}/
(H_{j+1}^{(a)}H_j^{(a+1)})$. All original mass factors are present
in the four determinants in this expression. Define only the scalar
notation $\lambda_j^{(a)}=\log(c_j^{(a)}/q^2)$, keeping the same $q$
for every shift $a$. HCT37 and the exact low identity PHT22 give
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 W_k=\widetilde P_{q,l}+\sum_{s=0}^{l-1}\bigg[
  &2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda_j^{(q+s)}+\lambda_n^{(q+s)}\\
  &+2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda_j^{(q+s+1)}
       -\lambda_0^{(q+s)}\bigg],\quad n=q/2.
 \end{aligned}}\tag{WGP11}
\]
To verify the entire scale cancellation, each bracket has
$2n+1+2n-1=4n=2q$ copies of $2\log q$ before this scalar
notation is introduced. Across the $l$ original shifts this is
$4lq\log q$, exactly the term recorded in WGP3. The two high ranks,
the doubled odd block and the subtracted low endpoint remain visible
in WGP11. The finite remainders in WGP10 therefore enter the same
$W_k$ as in LET19--21, with coefficient $+1$; in the arithmetic
receiver $B_{\rm ar}=B_0+\delta_\sigma-\mathfrak T_k$ their signs
reverse because the coefficient of $W_k$ is $-1$.

\paragraph{The constant-scale term obtained during the independent check.}
The following refinement retains the same source products and all their
endpoints. Integrating $1/(1+u)=1-u+u^2-u^3/(1+u)$ gives
\[
 \log(1+u)=u-u^2/2+u^3/3-r_4(u),\qquad
 r_4(u)=\int_0^u\frac{t^3}{1+t}\,dt,\quad0\le r_4(u)\le u^4/4.
\]
Write
\[
 A_3=8l^4+2l^2,\qquad
 A_4=\sum_{j<2l}(j^4+(j+1)^4)=\frac{192l^5+80l^3-2l}{15}.
 \tag{WGP12}
\]
To verify the last polynomial, set $N=2l$ and subtract its value
$2N^5/5+2N^3/3-N/15$ at $N$ from its value at $N+1$.
Expansion gives exactly $N^4+(N+1)^4$; at $N=0$ both sides vanish.
This proves the finite sum by induction. WGP4 at $x^{-3}$ and
the convex midpoint inequality at $x^{-4}$ give
\[
 \sum_bx_b^{-3}=3q/8-\varepsilon_3,\quad
 0\le\varepsilon_3\le(2q)^{-1},\qquad
 \sum_bx_b^{-4}\le7q/24.
\]
The derivatives and integrals used here are $(x^{-3})''=12x^{-5}$,
$\int_1^2x^{-3}dx=3/8$, and $\int_1^2x^{-4}dx=7/24$.
Thus the nonnegative quartic remainder $Q_4$ obeys
$0\le Q_4\le7A_4/(96q^3)$, and the exact refinement is
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 P_k={}&-4lq\log q-4lq(2\log2-1)-4l^2\log2
       +\frac{8l^3+l}{6q}-\frac{l^4+l^2/4}{q^2}
       +\mathcal E^{(3)}_{q,l},\\
 -\frac l{6q}-\frac{8l^3+l}{12q^3}
 \le{}&\mathcal E^{(3)}_{q,l}\le
 \frac{l^2}{3q^2}+\frac{A_3}{6q^4}+\frac{7A_4}{96q^3}.
 \end{aligned}}\tag{WGP13}
\]
Indeed its exact error is
\[
 \mathcal E^{(3)}_{q,l}=-4l\varepsilon_0
 +\frac{4l^2}{q}\varepsilon_1
 -\frac{8l^3+l}{3q^2}\varepsilon_2
 +\frac{A_3}{3q^3}\varepsilon_3+Q_4.
 \tag{WGP14}
\]
The cubic term has coefficient $-A_3(3q/8)/(3q^3)
=-(l^4+l^2/4)/q^2$, retaining its negative sign.
The displayed bounds give $\mathcal E^{(3)}_{q,l}=O(l^{-1})$
for $m=1$ and $O(l^{-2})$ for every fixed $m\ge2$.
For $m=1$ the earlier exact remainder therefore satisfies
$\mathcal E_{q,l}=-1/256+O(l^{-1})$, because $q=(4l+2)^2$.
WGP13 supersedes the coarser numerical expansion WGP10 when this
constant-scale information is used; WGP10 remains a valid independently
proved enclosure. Both versions enter the identical WGP11 and LET21
maps, with their already specified opposite arithmetic sign.

% BEGIN INLINE SOURCE 5; SHA256 69ddf10e4a479cb987215c51da094fb937e70891989c6ee386eeea757e15df46
% New complete growing-degree bound. No historical source is modified.
\section{A positive growing-degree bound for the complete Gamma return}
\label{sec:RWB}

Retain exactly the measure, monic polynomials, norms and Christoffel
coefficients of HCT1--24:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 d\sigma(y)=\frac{|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2}{2\pi}\,dy,\\
 \nu=(y\mapsto y^2)_*\sigma,
 \quad d\nu_a(t)=t^a\,d\nu(t),\\
 h_j^{(a)}=\int_0^\infty(P_j^{(a)}(t))^2d\nu_a(t),
 \quad c_j^{(a)}=\frac{h_j^{(a+1)}}{h_j^{(a)}}.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{RWB1}
\]
Every $P_j^{(a)}$ is monic of degree $j$. The original mass
$\sqrt{2\pi}$ remains in the norms; the last quotient is the exact HCT13
identity. Throughout this section $a,j$ are nonnegative integers, and
the estimates containing $4a-1$ require $a\ge1$.

\paragraph{The logarithmic derivative of the original Gamma density.}
Write $\sigma(y)$ also for the displayed density and set
$L(y)=-\sigma'(y)/\sigma(y)$. For every $y\in\mathbb R$,
\[
 -\frac12\le yL(y)-\frac\pi2|y|\le\frac32,
 \qquad
 \left|y\left(L(y)-\frac\pi2\operatorname{sgn}y\right)\right|
 \le\frac32.
 \tag{RWB2}
\]
The expressions at $y=0$ have their continuous value zero. Here is a
derivation retaining the exact parameter $1/4$. For $\Re z>0$,
integration by parts in the beta integral and the change $u=Nx$ give
\[
 \frac{N!N^z}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)}
 =\int_0^N u^{z-1}(1-u/N)^N\,du\longrightarrow\Gamma(z).
 \tag{RWB3}
\]
The inequality $(1-u/N)^N\le e^{-u}$ proves locally uniform convergence
on every compact subset of $\Re z>0$: near zero use the least real part
on that compact set, and above one use its greatest real part. The same
majorants with a factor $|\log u|$ justify differentiated convergence.
Nonvanishing at each $z=\alpha+iv$, $\alpha>0$, follows directly from
the same limits, since
\[
 \frac{|\Gamma(\alpha+iv)|}{\Gamma(\alpha)}
 =\lim_{N\to\infty}\prod_{r=0}^N
       \left(1+\frac{v^2}{(r+\alpha)^2}\right)^{-1/2}>0.
\]
The infinite product has a positive limit because
$\sum_r\log(1+v^2/(r+\alpha)^2)$ converges by comparison with
$\sum_r v^2/(r+\alpha)^2$. Thus logarithmic differentiation, with
$z=1/4+iv$, gives the convergent imaginary-part series
\[
 \operatorname{Im}\frac{\Gamma'(1/4+iv)}{\Gamma(1/4+iv)}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{v}{(r+1/4)^2+v^2},
 \qquad L(y)=\operatorname{Im}\frac{\Gamma'(1/4+iy/2)}{
                                  \Gamma(1/4+iy/2)}.
 \tag{RWB4}
\]
Only an imaginary part is taken, so the real $\log N$ term disappears.
For $v>0$ let $F_v(x)=v^2/(x^2+v^2)$, a positive decreasing function
on $[0,\infty)$. Integral comparison on each unit interval proves
\[
 \int_{1/4}^{\infty}F_v(x)\,dx
 \le\sum_{r\ge0}F_v(r+1/4)
 \le F_v(1/4)+\int_{1/4}^{\infty}F_v(x)\,dx.
\]
Since $\int_0^\infty F_v(x)\,dx=\pi v/2$, the difference between
the sum and this last integral is at least $-1/4$ and at most
\[
 F_v(1/4)-\int_0^{1/4}F_v(x)\,dx
 \le\frac34F_v(1/4)\le\frac34.
\]
For $y=2v>0$, RWB4 gives $yL(y)=2\sum_r F_v(r+1/4)$.
Multiplying the preceding interval by two proves RWB2 at positive $y$.
Evenness of $\sigma$ makes $yL(y)$ even, proving the assertion on the
whole original real line.

All integrations by parts below are justified by the exponential bound
proved from the Euler contour in HCT1. Formula RWB4 shows that $L$ is
continuous at zero; RWB2 bounds it at infinity, so the differentiated
integrands have the required integrability as well.

\paragraph{An inverse-square estimate with its exact polynomial space.}
For $a\ge1$ fix $Q=P_j^{(a+1)}$ and put
\[
 J_{a,j}=
 \frac{\int_0^\infty Q(t)^2\,d\nu_a(t)}
      {\int_0^\infty Q(t)^2\,d\nu_{a+1}(t)}.
\]
Then
\[
 0<J_{a,j}\le\left(\frac{\pi}{4a-1}\right)^2.
 \tag{RWB5}
\]
To prove this, the exact density of $\nu_{a+1}$ from HCT1 is
$t^{a+1/2}\sigma(\sqrt t)$ on $(0,\infty)$. Integrate the derivative
of $Q(t)^2t^{a+1/2}\sigma(\sqrt t)$ over that interval. Its boundary
values vanish: at zero $a+1/2>0$, and at infinity the factor has a
polynomial times exponential bound. The term $2QQ'$ integrates to
zero because $\deg Q'<j$ and $Q$ is orthogonal in the exact measure
$\nu_{a+1}$. Dividing by $h_j^{(a+1)}$ therefore gives
\[
 (a+1/2)J_{a,j}
 =\frac12\,\frac{\int_0^\infty
                 Q(t)^2 L(\sqrt t)/\sqrt t\,d\nu_{a+1}(t)}
                    {h_j^{(a+1)}}.
 \tag{RWB6}
\]
RWB2 implies $L(\sqrt t)/\sqrt t\le
\pi/(2\sqrt t)+3/(2t)$. Cauchy--Schwarz in the same positive measure
gives an upper bound $\sqrt{J_{a,j}}$ for the ratio obtained by
inserting $t^{-1/2}$. Consequently
\[
 (a-1/4)J_{a,j}\le\frac\pi4\sqrt{J_{a,j}}.
\]
The denominator is positive and $J_{a,j}>0$, so division proves RWB5.
No assertion concerning an unspecified inverse operator is used: both
integrals defining $J_{a,j}$ are the original polynomial integrals.

\paragraph{A lower bound including both the degree and the shift.}
For every $a\ge1,j\ge0$,
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \sqrt{c_j^{(a)}}\ \ge\
 \frac{2(4a-1)(2a+2j+1)}{\pi(4a+2)}
 =\frac4\pi\,(a+j+1/2)\frac{4a-1}{4a+2}.}
 \tag{RWB7}
\]
Here the square root is the positive one. Work in the original
real-line measure $dw_a(y)=y^{2a}d\sigma(y)$ and define
\[
 R(y)=P_j^{(a)}(y^2),\qquad
 O(y)=yP_j^{(a+1)}(y^2).
 \tag{RWB8}
\]
Evenness of $w_a$ and the defining orthogonality on the square
pushforward show that $R,O$ are the monic orthogonal polynomials of
degrees $2j,2j+1$ in $L^2(w_a)$. Their norms are exactly
$h_j^{(a)}$ and $h_j^{(a+1)}$; both parity sectors have been retained.
In particular
\[
 \int O'R\,dw_a=(2j+1)h_j^{(a)},\qquad
 \int OR'\,dw_a=0,\qquad
 \int\frac{OR}{y}\,dw_a=h_j^{(a)}.
 \tag{RWB9}
\]
The last integrand is a polynomial: $O/y=P_j^{(a+1)}(y^2)$ is monic
of the same degree as $R$, so their difference has lower degree.
Integrating $(ORy^{2a}\sigma)'$ and using RWB9 proves the exact identity
\[
 2a+2j+1=\frac{\int OR L\,dw_a}{h_j^{(a)}}.
 \tag{RWB10}
\]
At zero the boundary term is zero because $O$ is odd, and the apparent
$1/y$ term has just been exhibited as a polynomial. At both infinite
ends exponential decay proves vanishing.

Write $L(y)=(\pi/2)\operatorname{sgn}y+\rho(y)$.
Cauchy--Schwarz bounds the first contribution in RWB10 by
$(\pi/2)\sqrt{c_j^{(a)}}$. For the second, $|y\rho(y)|\le3/2$
from RWB2 and $O/y=P_j^{(a+1)}(y^2)$ give the bound
\[
 \frac32\sqrt{
 \frac{\int(P_j^{(a+1)}(y^2))^2\,dw_a}{h_j^{(a)}}}
 =\frac32\sqrt{c_j^{(a)}J_{a,j}}
 \le\frac{3\pi}{2(4a-1)}\sqrt{c_j^{(a)}}.
\]
Thus $2a+2j+1\le(\pi/2)(4a+2)/(4a-1)\sqrt{c_j^{(a)}}$,
which is RWB7, including its original degree and constants.

\paragraph{The low coefficient with the sign needed in the return.}
For $p\ge0$ write $I_p=\int_0^\infty y^p\sigma(y)\,dy>0$.
Integration of $(y^{p+1}\sigma(y))'$ and RWB2 give
\[
 \frac{2p-1}{\pi}\le\frac{I_{p+1}}{I_p}
 \le\frac{2p+3}{\pi}.
 \tag{RWB11}
\]
For $p<1/2$ the lower bound is simply a valid negative bound; it will
not be used there. Indeed the integrated identity is
$(p+1)I_p=\int y^{p+1}L(y)\sigma(y)\,dy$; substituting the interval
in RWB2 proves both signs in RWB11. Since $P_0^{(a)}=1$, evenness
gives $c_0^{(a)}=I_{2a+2}/I_{2a}$. Multiplying two upper bounds in
RWB11 proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle c_0^{(a)}\le
 \frac{(4a+3)(4a+5)}{\pi^2}.}
 \tag{RWB12}
\]
This is the upper bound appropriate to the negative low-endpoint term;
RWB7 will be used only in the positively signed high terms.

\paragraph{The full four-endpoint functional at the original ranks.}
Retain the packet integers
\[
 k=4l+1,\quad l\ge1,\quad m\ge1,\quad
 e=1+k(m-1),\quad q=e(k+1)^2=2n.
 \tag{RWB13}
\]
Let $W_k$ be exactly LET19. With
\[
 G=\prod_{b=0}^{2l-1}
 \left\{\prod_{u=q}^{2q-1}(u+b+1/2)
         \prod_{u=q+1}^{2q}(u+b+1/2)\right\},
\]
HCT37 and the low moment ratio give the exact identity
\[
 e^{W_k}=\frac1G\prod_{s=0}^{l-1}
 \frac{\displaystyle
       \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s)}\right)^2
       c_n^{(q+s)}
       \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j^{(q+s+1)}\right)^2}
      {c_0^{(q+s)}}.
 \tag{RWB14}
\]
Here $P_k=-\log G$ follows by cancelling the overlapping literal
products $D_{l,N}$ in LET19, with both endpoint intervals as displayed.
The source masses cancel only in these prescribed equal-dimension
ratios. There are $4lq$ original linear factors in $G$. In each $s$
factor the net number of $c$ coefficients is $2q$. Therefore division
of each linear factor by $q$ and each $c$ by $q^2$ cancels exactly
$4lq\log q$ on the two sides and yields
\begin{align}
 W_k={}&-\sum_{b=0}^{2l-1}
 \left[\sum_{u=q}^{2q-1}\log\frac{u+b+1/2}{q}
       +\sum_{u=q+1}^{2q}\log\frac{u+b+1/2}{q}\right]\notag\\
 &+\sum_{s=0}^{l-1}\left[
 2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{c_j^{(q+s)}}{q^2}
 +\log\frac{c_n^{(q+s)}}{q^2}
 +2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{c_j^{(q+s+1)}}{q^2}
 -\log\frac{c_0^{(q+s)}}{q^2}\right].
 \tag{RWB15}
\end{align}
This is an exact equality of the original quantities, with every scale
factor accounted for, rather than replacement of the original objects.

Define $r_q=(4q-1)/(4q+2)$. The function
$a\mapsto(4a-1)/(4a+2)$ is increasing for $a\ge1$. RWB7 thus implies,
for both $a=q+s$ and $a=q+s+1$,
\[
 \log\frac{c_j^{(a)}}{q^2}
 \ge2\log\frac4\pi+2\log(1+j/q)+2\log r_q.
 \tag{RWB16}
\]
RWB12 and $s\le l-1$ give the simultaneous upper bound
\[
 \frac{c_0^{(q+s)}}{q^2}\le
 U_{q,l}:=\frac{(4q+4l-1)(4q+4l+1)}{\pi^2q^2}
 \le\left(\frac4\pi\right)^2
          \left(1+\frac{l+1/4}{q}\right)^2.
 \tag{RWB17}
\]
For the increasing function $x\mapsto\log(1+x)$, comparison of its
left and right sums on $[0,1/2]$ gives
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log(1+j/q)
 \ge q\int_0^{1/2}\log(1+x)\,dx-\log(3/2)
 =q\left[\frac32\log(3/2)-\frac12\right]-\log(3/2).
 \tag{RWB18}
\]
Inserting RWB16--18 into the $s$ bracket of RWB15, counting its
$2q+1$ positive coefficients, gives the lower bound
\[
 4q\left[\log(4/\pi)+3\log(3/2)-1\right]
 +2\log(4/\pi)+(4q+2)\log r_q
 -6\log(3/2)-\log U_{q,l}.
 \tag{RWB19}
\]

For the first line of RWB15 use
$\log((u+b+1/2)/q)\le\log(u/q)+(b+1/2)/u
\le\log(u/q)+(b+1/2)/q$. The sum of the latter corrections over
both $u$ intervals and all $b$ is exactly $4l^2$.
Concavity of $\log x$ shows that its composite trapezoidal sum on
$[1,2]$ is at most its integral: each chord lies below the graph.
Consequently
\[
 \sum_{u=q}^{2q-1}\log(u/q)+\sum_{u=q+1}^{2q}\log(u/q)
 \le2q\int_1^2\log x\,dx=2q(2\log2-1).
\]
The first line of RWB15 is therefore at least
$-4lq(2\log2-1)-4l^2$. Add $l$ copies of RWB19 and use
\[
 (4q+2)\log r_q\ge-\frac{3(4q+2)}{4q-1}
 =-3-\frac9{4q-1},\qquad
 \log(1+x)\le x\quad(x\ge0).
\]
The first inequality follows by integrating
$1/(1-t)\le1/(1-x)$ on $0\le t\le x<1$.
This proves the completely explicit bound
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 W_k\ge4lq\log\frac{27}{8\pi}
       -4l^2-6l\log(3/2)-3l
       -\frac{9l}{4q-1}-\frac{2l(l+1/4)}q.}
 \tag{RWB20}
\]
Every term is at the original finite $q,l$, including the negatively
signed low coefficient. In particular no large-rank value of a Hankel
determinant or a recurrence coefficient has been assumed.

\paragraph{Uniform positivity and the actual packet limit.}
The preceding finite bound implies
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle W_k\ge\frac{lq}{64}>0
 \quad\text{for every }l\ge1,\ m\ge1.}
 \tag{RWB21}
\]
Here are rational verifications of the constants. The identity
\[
 \frac{22}{7}-\pi=\int_0^1
                \frac{x^4(1-x)^4}{1+x^2}\,dx>0
\]
follows by polynomial division and $\int_0^1(1+x^2)^{-1}dx=\pi/4$.
Thus $27/(8\pi)>189/176$. For $x\ge0$, differentiation proves
$\log(1+x)\ge2x/(2+x)$, and hence
$4\log(27/(8\pi))>104/365$. Integrating
$1/(1+t)\le1-t+t^2$ over $0\le t\le1/2$ gives
$\log(3/2)\le5/12$.
RWB13 gives $q\ge(4l+2)^2\ge36l$; the second inequality is
$(4l+2)^2-36l=4(4l-1)(l-1)\ge0$. Dividing RWB20 by $lq$ now yields
\[
 \frac{W_k}{lq}\ge
 \frac{104}{365}-\frac19-\frac{11}{72}-\frac1{572}
                    -\frac5{2592}
 =\frac{2349349}{135289440}
 =\frac1{64}+\frac{470903}{270578880}>\frac1{64}.
 \tag{RWB22}
\]
The four subtracted rational terms bound respectively
$4l/q$, $(6\log(3/2)+3)/q$, $9/[q(4q-1)]$, and
$2(l+1/4)/q^2$. This proves RWB21 on the entire stated packet domain.

Finally, along the original $k=4l+1\to\infty$ and any integer choices
$m=m(l)\ge1$, RWB13 gives $l/q\to0$ and $1/q\to0$.
The exact finite remainder in RWB20 therefore proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \liminf_{l\to\infty}\frac{W_{4l+1}}{lq}
 \ge4\log\frac{27}{8\pi}>0.}
 \tag{RWB23}
\]
This is a lower bound, with its finite remainder RWB20. No equality
or numerical asymptotic value is asserted. The exact original receiving
identities LET20--21 may now use this bound with their already proved
signs and errors; the arithmetic contribution in those identities is
left exactly as defined there.

\paragraph{A matching order bound from the original polynomial operator.}
For completeness, the exact original real-line recurrence HCT6 gives,
for $a\ge1,j\ge0$ and $D=a+2j$,
\[
 c_j^{(a)}\le
 \left[\sqrt{(D+1)(D+1/2)}+\sqrt{D(D-1/2)}\right]^2
 \le(2a+4j+1/2)^2.
 \tag{RWB24}
\]
Indeed the norm of a monic orthogonal polynomial is the minimum norm
among all monic polynomials of that degree: subtracting the orthogonal
polynomial leaves a lower-degree polynomial perpendicular to it.
Consequently
\[
 h_j^{(a+1)}\le\int_0^\infty
                  t(P_j^{(a)}(t))^2\,d\nu_a(t)
 =\|yF\|_{L^2(\sigma)}^2,\qquad
 F(y)=y^aP_j^{(a)}(y^2),\quad
 \|F\|_{L^2(\sigma)}^2=h_j^{(a)}.
\]
This is the explicit multiplication map on the original polynomials of
degree at most $D$, with image degree at most $D+1$.
For the exact orthonormal basis $e_d=p_d/\sqrt{\gamma_0d!(1/2)_d}$
from HCT5, its raising and lowering parts have coefficients
$\sqrt{(d+1)(d+1/2)}$ and $\sqrt{d(d-1/2)}$, respectively.
Their norms on degrees at most $D$ are at most the two terms in
RWB24; this follows by summing their mutually orthogonal images
separately. The triangle inequality proves the first estimate.
Since $D\ge1$, the arithmetic--geometric mean bounds on the two
square roots are $D+3/4$ and $D-1/4$, proving the second.

Combining the two lower bounds in RWB11, at $p=2a,2a+1$, also proves
\[
 c_0^{(a)}\ge\frac{(4a-1)(4a+1)}{\pi^2}\quad(a\ge1).
 \tag{RWB25}
\]
At the indices in RWB15, $a\le q+l$ and $j\le q/2$, so RWB24 gives
$c_j^{(a)}/q^2\le[4+(2l+1/2)/q]^2$. RWB25 gives
$c_0^{(q+s)}/q^2\ge(16-q^{-2})/\pi^2>1$, using $q\ge36$
and $\pi<22/7$. Every logarithm in the first line of RWB15 is
positive. Hence
\[
 W_k\le l(4q+2)\log\left(4+\frac{2l+1/2}{q}\right)
 \le6lq.
 \tag{RWB26}
\]
For the last numerical bound, $q\ge36l$ gives
$4+(2l+1/2)/q\le293/72$.
The positive exponential series gives
\[
 e^{10/7}\ge\sum_{r=0}^4\frac{(10/7)^r}{r!}
 =\frac{29593}{7203}>\frac{293}{72},
\]
where the rational inequality follows by cross multiplication.
Thus the logarithm is less than $10/7$ and
$(4q+2)10/7\le6q$ for $q\ge10$.
Together RWB21 and RWB26 prove the finite two-sided order estimate
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle \frac{lq}{64}\le W_k\le6lq
 \qquad(l\ge1,\ m\ge1).}
 \tag{RWB27}
\]
The narrower lower remainder RWB20, rather than the coarse lower
constant in RWB27, remains available for propagation into each
original receiving formula.
