\subsection*{Retained proof provider: GSR\_JQR}

% BEGIN EXACT REQUIRED PROOF GSR_JQR; SHA256 62aacd3d67a7a3ef882001f7494c6f4df1532f34d45865c65bc66b0df8b592f8
% Complete unmodified GSR and JQR bodies, with their source hashes.
% GSR SHA256 dd479f0d9b414c4ff05d9264f468c28ccc0a1499dabfff8c247aec67c04a1a24
% JQR SHA256 3feb792678bfbf66edcf1e22f9dfe2b1a0a9cd03fe8b39bd7c48d5c6c66ac9f7
\section{The signed Gamma return through its complete relation subspaces}
\label{sec:gsr-signed-gamma}

Retain exactly GCR1--GCR5: $k=4l+1$, $l\geq1$,
$c=k/2$, $e_k=1+k(m-1)$, $q=e_k(k+1)^2$, and
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \lambda_{a,b}=c+(2a-k)\delta+i(2b-k)\gamma,\quad
 \chi(S)=\prod_{a,b=0}^k(S-\lambda_{a,b})^{e_k},\\
 0<\delta<\tfrac12,\quad\gamma>2,\quad S=c+iy,\qquad
 f(S)=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(S-c-t_j),\quad t_j=2j+\tfrac12,\\
 d\sigma(y)=|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2\,dy/(2\pi),\qquad
 dm_{0,k}=\beta_k|f(c+iy)|^2d\sigma,\quad
 \beta_k=(2\pi)^{k/2}/(\sqrt2\,\Gamma(k/2)).
\end{gathered}\tag{GSR1}
\]
The original $h$, $v_h=(2\xi)/h$, complete Taylor unit,
remainder map $J_{\chi,N}$ and observation $\Lambda$ are the
unchanged ones of GCR1--GCR2 and GCR41--GCR44.
Let
\[
 N_0=q-1,\quad N_1=q,\quad N_2=2q-1,\quad N_3=2q,
 \qquad(\epsilon_0,\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2,\epsilon_3)=(1,1,-1,-1).
\]
The object calculated below is precisely
$\mathfrak T_k=\sum_a\epsilon_a
(\log\det G_{N_a}^0-\log\det G_{N_a}^\sigma)$
from GCR26. No source mass or packet coordinate is changed.

\subsection{The two exact low endpoints}

Use the full source determinant product and source bound
\[
\begin{gathered}
 D_{l,N}=\prod_{a=0}^N\prod_{r=0}^{2l-1}(a+r+\tfrac12),\qquad
 \mathscr U_{l,N}=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}
                         \bigl(4(N+j+1)^2+t_j^2\bigr),\\
 X_{l,N}=(N+1)\log\mathscr U_{l,N}-\log D_{l,N}\geq0.
\end{gathered}\tag{GSR2}
\]
These are exactly GCR28--GCR30. Put
$A_N=\log\det G_N^f-\log\det G_N^\sigma$, where $G_N^0=\beta_kG_N^f$.
For $N\geq q-1$ let
\[
 r_N=N+1-q,\qquad
 R_N=
 \frac{\det\operatorname{Gram}_\sigma
                 (f\chi,f\chi S,\ldots,f\chi S^{r_N-1})}
      {\det\operatorname{Gram}_\sigma
                 (\chi,\chi S,\ldots,\chi S^{r_N-1})}.
\tag{GSR3}
\]
Both empty determinants at $r_N=0$ are one. GCR19 proves
the exact identity
\[
 A_N=\log D_{l,N}-\log R_N.
\tag{GSR4}
\]
In particular, define the full original moment ratio
\[
 \mathfrak r_\chi=
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}
              |f(c+iy)\chi(c+iy)|^2\,d\sigma(y)}
      {\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb R}
              |\chi(c+iy)|^2\,d\sigma(y)}>0.
\tag{GSR5}
\]
Then
\[
 A_{q-1}=\log D_{l,q-1},\qquad
 A_q=\log D_{l,q}-\log\mathfrak r_\chi.
\tag{GSR6}
\]
The first source has no relation directions and the second
has the single original direction $\chi$. This is an equality,
not an estimate which treats either kernel as having growing rank.
All moments are finite, since the original Gamma density has
exponential tails and both polynomials have finite degree.
Pointwise $|f(c+iy)|^2=\prod_j(y^2+t_j^2)\geq\prod_jt_j^2$;
the original polynomial bound GCR29 on the degree-$q$ input
$\chi$ proves
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{l-1}t_j^2\leq\mathfrak r_\chi\leq\mathscr U_{l,q}.
\tag{GSR7}
\]
Consequently $|\log\mathfrak r_\chi|=O_m(k\log(k+2))$ along
the stated degrees. For example the upper logarithm in GSR7
is bounded by
$l\log(4(q+l)^2+4l^2)$, and the lower logarithm is at least
$-\log4$, since $t_0^2=1/4$ and $t_j^2>1$ for $j\geq1$.
In particular $\log\mathfrak r_\chi/(kq)\to0$.

The four copies of $q\log\beta_k$ cancel with their original
signs. Equations GSR4--GSR6 therefore give
\[
\begin{gathered}
 C_k=\log D_{l,q-1}+\log D_{l,q}-\log\mathfrak r_\chi
                     -\log D_{l,2q-1}-\log D_{l,2q},\\
 \boxed{\quad \mathfrak T_k=C_k+\log R_{2q-1}+\log R_{2q}.\quad}
\end{gathered}
\tag{GSR8}
\]
Thus the two high-degree determinants in this formula are the
complete original relation determinants.

\subsection{A finite Jacobi realization with the original source metric}

Let $b_n(y)$ and $\gamma_n$ be exactly GCR11, and let
$e_n(y)=b_n(y)/\sqrt{\gamma_n}$. For a positive integer $D$
define the real symmetric matrix $\mathcal J_D$ on coordinates
$0,\ldots,D-1$ by
\[
 (\mathcal J_D)_{a-1,a}=(\mathcal J_D)_{a,a-1}
       =\sqrt{a(a-\tfrac12)}\quad(1\leq a<D),
 \qquad(\mathcal J_D)_{a,a}=0.
\tag{GSR9}
\]
For $P\in\mathcal P_{D-1}$ in the original $S$ coordinate,
write
\[
 P(c+iy)=\sum_{n=0}^{D-1}z_n e_n(y),\qquad
 \mathcal C_D(P)=(z_0,\ldots,z_{D-1})^t .
\tag{GSR10}
\]
This is an explicit linear isometry from the original polynomial
source with its $\sigma$ norm to $\mathbb C^D$ with its Euclidean
norm. Its inverse is the polynomial
$S\mapsto\sum_nz_ne_n((S-c)/i)$. The orthonormal coordinate
frame has not altered $d\sigma$ or its mass $\gamma_0=\sqrt{2\pi}$.
The recurrence proves
$\mathcal C_D(((S-c)/i)P)=\mathcal J_D\mathcal C_D(P)$
whenever $\deg P\leq D-2$.

At a fixed endpoint $N$ put
\[
 D=N+l+1,\qquad
 \Phi_l(x)=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(x^2+t_j^2),\qquad
 F_l(x)=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(x+it_j).
\tag{GSR11}
\]
Every intermediate product of degree at most $l$ with an input
in $\mathcal P_N$ has degree at most $N+l=D-1$. Applying the
recurrence successively before reaching that last degree gives
the exact multiplication identity
\[
 \mathcal C_D(fP)=i^l F_l(\mathcal J_D)\mathcal C_D(P)
       \quad(P\in\mathcal P_N).
\tag{GSR12}
\]
Since $\mathcal J_D$ is self-adjoint,
$F_l(\mathcal J_D)^*F_l(\mathcal J_D)=\Phi_l(\mathcal J_D)>0$.
This equality concerns the squared norm of the exact degree-$l$
map in GSR12; it does not assert that multiplication by a
degree-$2l$ polynomial has no truncation on every source vector.

For the original relation space put
\[
 Z_N=
 \big(\mathcal C_D(\chi),\mathcal C_D(\chi S),\ldots,
                         \mathcal C_D(\chi S^{r_N-1})\big),\qquad
 B_N=Z_N(Z_N^*Z_N)^{-1/2}.
\tag{GSR13}
\]
At $r_N>0$ its columns are independent: cancellation of the
nonzero monic $\chi$ proves independence of the original
polynomials, and GSR10 is injective. Hence $B_N^*B_N=I_{r_N}$.
The exact relation determinant becomes
\[
 \boxed{\quad R_N=\det\bigl(B_N^*\Phi_l(\mathcal J_D)B_N\bigr).\quad}
\tag{GSR14}
\]
Indeed its denominator in GSR3 is $\det(Z_N^*Z_N)$; its
numerator, by GSR12, is
$\det(Z_N^*\Phi_l(\mathcal J_D)Z_N)$.
This proves GSR14 with the full relation subspace present.

The characteristic polynomial of $\mathcal J_D$ is $b_D$.
Expansion of the tridiagonal determinant along its last row
gives the monic recurrence with initial values $1,y$, exactly
GCR11. Its $D$ eigenvalues $x_{D,i}$ are real and simple.
To prove simplicity, the first coordinate of any eigenvector
determines the second by the first row, and then determines
every subsequent coordinate by the nonzero off-diagonal
recurrence; a zero first coordinate forces the whole vector
to vanish. Each eigenspace thus has dimension one, and
self-adjointness gives a full orthonormal eigenbasis.
Let
\[
 0<\pi_{D,1}\leq\cdots\leq\pi_{D,D}
\]
be the eigenvalues of $\Phi_l(\mathcal J_D)$, with multiplicity.
They are exactly the $D$ positive numbers
$\Phi_l(x_{D,i})$, ordered by $|x_{D,i}|$.
In its orthonormal eigenbasis the Cauchy--Binet formula gives
\[
 R_N=\sum_{\substack{I\subset\{1,\ldots,D\}\\|I|=r_N}}
       w_{N,I}\prod_{i\in I}\pi_{D,i},\qquad
 w_{N,I}\geq0,\quad\sum_Iw_{N,I}=1.
\tag{GSR15}
\]
Here $w_{N,I}$ is the squared determinant of the rows $I$
of the actual matrix $B_N$ in that eigenbasis. The last equality
is Cauchy--Binet applied to $B_N^*B_N=I$.
It follows that the finite spectral bounds are
\[
 s^-_{l,N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{r_N}\log\pi_{D,i}
       \leq\log R_N
       \leq\sum_{i=D-r_N+1}^D\log\pi_{D,i}
                       =:s^+_{l,N}.
\tag{GSR16}
\]
Empty sums are zero. All eigenvalues here come from the explicitly
specified original recurrence matrix; no packet-limit model has
been substituted for it.

\subsection{The actual positive weights and the original mixed determinant}

The weights in GSR15 can be written explicitly, so that its
comparison is tied to the complete original packet. Let $v_i$
be a real unit eigenvector of $\mathcal J_D$ at $x_{D,i}$,
with first coordinate positive, and put
\[
 \omega_{D,i}=\gamma_0(v_i)_0^2>0,\qquad
                    \sum_i\omega_{D,i}=\gamma_0.
\tag{GSR17}
\]
The eigenvector recurrence gives
$(v_i)_n=\sqrt{\omega_{D,i}}\,e_n(x_{D,i})$.
It follows directly from GSR10 that
$v_i^*\mathcal C_D(P)=
\sqrt{\omega_{D,i}}\,P(c+ix_{D,i})$ for every
$P\in\mathcal P_{D-1}$. This proves the evaluation formula
with its original mass, without introducing a probability
normalization of the source.

Order a subset $I$ by its distinct real nodes. The corresponding
minor of $Z_N$ is a product of the evaluation values of $\chi$,
the square roots of $\omega_{D,i}$, and the Vandermonde
determinant of $c+ix_{D,i}$. Thus
\[
 w_{N,I}=
 \frac{\displaystyle
   \prod_{i\in I}\omega_{D,i}|\chi(c+ix_{D,i})|^2
   \prod_{\substack{i<j\\i,j\in I}}(x_{D,j}-x_{D,i})^2}
 {\displaystyle
   \det\operatorname{Gram}_\sigma
               (\chi,\chi S,\ldots,\chi S^{r_N-1})}.
\tag{GSR18}
\]
The absolute value of the Vandermonde phase
$i^{r_N(r_N-1)/2}$ is one. Its original coordinate shift $c$
cancels in the differences, not in the original columns.
Every packet factor in this formula is explicitly
\[
 |\chi(c+ix)|^2
  =\prod_{a,b=0}^k
     \left(((2a-k)\delta)^2+
                    (x-(2b-k)\gamma)^2\right)^{e_k}>0.
\tag{GSR19}
\]
Positivity uses that $2a-k$ is nonzero for this odd $k$.
Thus every weight in GSR18 is strictly positive when $r_N>0$.
The complete multiplicity $e_k$ and every original mixed support
point enter this exact formula.

The weights sum to one because they are squared minors of
an isometric frame. They are coefficients in a finite
determinant identity, not replacement source masses. In
particular the original quadrature weights in GSR17 retain
their total $\sqrt{2\pi}$.

For a direct connection to the complete mixed matrix of GCR26,
let $U_f$ be the full jet multiplication matrix in GCR7,
and let $d_N$ be precisely GCR23. Subtracting the fixed
quotient formula in GCR25 and using GSR4 yields
\[
 \log d_N
     =2\log|\det U_f|-\log D_{l,N}+\log R_N.
\tag{GSR20}
\]
Every matrix in GSR14--GSR18 therefore maps to that same
$2l$ determinant by this exact scalar identity. The
degree-independent unit factor cancels only in the four signed
terms, giving GSR8 and GCR26 with identical values.
No mixed term in the positive factorization GCR24 has been
dropped; GSR20 computes its full determinant.

\subsection{A strictly narrower finite signed enclosure}

The scalar source inequality GCR29 and the determinant
compression argument GCR31--GCR32 also give
\[
 \log D_{l,N}-q\log\mathscr U_{l,N}
    \leq\log R_N\leq r_N\log\mathscr U_{l,N}.
\tag{GSR21}
\]
The lower side follows by applying
$\log\det G_N^f-\log\det G_N^\sigma\leq
q\log\mathscr U_{l,N}$ in GSR4.
The upper side is the direct determinant inequality on the
actual relation space, which has dimension $r_N$.
The pointwise lower bound for $|f|^2$ gives in addition
$\log R_N\geq r_N\sum_j\log t_j^2$.
Define the following entirely finite numbers:
\[
\begin{split}
 L_{l,N}
 &=\max\left\{s^-_{l,N},\
     \log D_{l,N}-q\log\mathscr U_{l,N},\
                       r_N\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\log t_j^2\right\},\\
 U_{l,N}
 &=\min\left\{s^+_{l,N},\
                         r_N\log\mathscr U_{l,N}\right\}.
\end{split}\tag{GSR22}
\]
All entries are explicit products or eigenvalues of GSR9;
the full original moment in $C_k$ remains exactly GSR5.
Equations GSR8, GSR16 and GSR21 prove
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 C_k+L_{l,2q-1}+L_{l,2q}
       \leq\mathfrak T_k
       \leq C_k+U_{l,2q-1}+U_{l,2q}.
 \quad}\tag{GSR23}
\]
This interval is contained in GCR33. Indeed at each high
endpoint GSR22 includes both bounds used to prove GCR33;
at the low endpoints GSR6 supplies their exact values instead
of the two ranges used there.
Its upper side is strictly smaller for $l\geq1$.
To check this without relying on a spectral approximation,
the high upper choices in GSR22 give
\[
 C_k+U_{l,2q-1}+U_{l,2q}
 \leq A_{q-1}+A_q
       -q\log\mathscr U_{l,2q-1}
       -q\log\mathscr U_{l,2q}
       +X_{l,2q-1}+X_{l,2q}.
\]
In contrast, the upper side of GCR33 replaces
$A_{q-1}+A_q$ by
$q\log\mathscr U_{l,q-1}+q\log\mathscr U_{l,q}$.
Their difference is at least $X_{l,q-1}>0$.
For strict positivity, the row-sum estimate in GCR29 is strict
on the constant input at the first multiplication step:
$\|y\|_\sigma^2/\|1\|_\sigma^2=1/2<4q^2$ at $N=q-1$.
The later factors are positive and their stated upper bounds
remain valid, so the full source inequality has a strictly
smaller Rayleigh quotient on that nonzero vector.
Since all its relative eigenvalues are at most one, its
determinant is strictly smaller than one. This is exactly
$X_{l,q-1}>0$.

\subsection{The complete source and observation maps in the refinement}

No relation kernel has been inferred from the sizes of the
comparison matrices. Its actual coefficient injection is
the map $z\mapsto\chi\sum_{a=0}^{r_N-1}z_aS^a$ in GSR13.
The isometry GSR10 takes that injection to $Z_N$;
multiplication by $f$ takes it, by GSR12, to
$i^lF_l(\mathcal J_D)Z_N$. Its two Grams are exactly the
numerator and denominator of GSR3. Hence GSR14 is a proved
map on the full original source relation.

For the original remainder fibre let
$R_N^f=(H_N^f)^{-1}J_{\chi,N}^*G_N^f$.
The same source minimum argument as GCR14 gives
$J_{\chi,N}R_N^f=I$ and
\[
 \ker(\Lambda J_{\chi,N})
    =\chi\mathcal P_{N-q}\ \mathbin{\perp_{H_N^f}}\
                         R_N^f(\ker\Lambda).
\tag{GSR24}
\]
The first summand is sent to the exact matrix image in the
preceding paragraph, and the second is sent to
$i^lF_l(\mathcal J_D)\mathcal C_D R_N^f(\ker\Lambda)$.
Their orthogonality follows from the source equality GSR12.
The ambient product-jet observation remains
$(0,Y)\mapsto\Lambda\mathcal E_\chi^{-1}U_f^{-1}Y$
from GCR44; the isometry GSR10 and its displayed inverse
make its composition with the finite Jacobi coordinates
explicit. Thus the refinement preserves the full original
unit, nilpotents, source relation and proper observation kernel.

The moving bounds for the same finite spectral products are
proved in the adjoining Jacobi quantile calculation. They
are obtained from the original moving finite matrices,
not from a fixed-packet kernel limit.

\subsection{The resulting moving signed constants}

The complete finite proof JQR1--JQR39, included with this source,
gives an elementary counting law for the same matrices in GSR9.
Define the exact number $a\in(0,2)$ and the exact integral $I$ by
\[
 a\operatorname{arcosh}(2/a)+2\arcsin(a/2)=\pi/2,\qquad
 I=\frac1\pi\int_a^2\log x\,\operatorname{arcosh}(2/x)\,dx.
\tag{GSR25}
\]
Existence and uniqueness of $a$ follow from the strictly
increasing function in JQR13--JQR14. It is the middle absolute
eigenvalue cutoff of the proved limiting counting measure,
not a new parameter of the original quartet.
The positive and negative finite products in GSR16 satisfy
at exactly $N=2q-1,2q$, with $D=N+l+1$, $r_N=N+1-q$,
\[
\begin{split}
 s^+_{l,N}
     &=2lr_N\log D+2lD I+o(lD),\\
 s^-_{l,N}
     &=2lr_N\log D-2lD(1+I)+o(lD).
\end{split}\tag{GSR26}
\]
Here JQR22 is an explicit finite upper-product error;
JQR34 and JQR38--JQR39 give explicit finite full- and
lower-product errors. In particular no lower-tail logarithm
is inferred from weak convergence near a zero eigenvalue.
The auxiliary spectral coordinate $x_{D,i}/D$ is returned
to the original eigenvalue by every factor of $D$ in GSR26.

To verify the moving domains, the two pairs are precisely
\[
 (D,r_N)=(2q+l,q),\quad(2q+l+1,q+1).
\tag{GSR27}
\]
They obey $r_N/D\to1/2$, $l/D\to0$ and
$l\log(2(D+l))/D\to0$, since
$q=e_k(k+1)^2\geq(k+1)^2$ and $q=O_m(k^3)$.
These are the domains required in the fully finite JQR
proof. Both upper products and their complementary ranks
are thereby controlled at these actual degrees.

For explicit cancellation of the growing elementary terms,
GCR35 says
\[
 \log D_{l,N}=2l(N+1)(\log(N+1)-1)+\theta_{l,N+1},
\]
where its displayed bound for $\theta$ is
$O(l^2\log(N+2))$ on these degrees. GSR7 gives
$\log\mathfrak r_\chi=o(lq)$.
Using $N_a+1=q,q+1,2q,2q+1$ in GSR8 yields
\[
 \frac{C_k}{2lq}
          =-2\log q-4\log2+2+o(1).
\tag{GSR28}
\]
For instance replacing $(q+1)\log(q+1)$ by $q\log q$
costs $O(\log(q+1))$ by the mean value theorem; after
division by $q$ this tends to zero. The same reasoning
handles $2q+1$, and the finite $\theta$ errors divided
by $lq$ are $O(l\log q/q)\to0$. Thus the equality
retains, and then cancels, the terms in $\log q$.

The two upper-product expansions in GSR26 give
\[
 \frac{s^+_{l,2q-1}+s^+_{l,2q}}{2lq}
             =2\log q+2\log2+4I+o(1).
\tag{GSR29}
\]
Indeed $D/q\to2$, the two ranks are $q,q+1$, and
$D=2q+O(l)$, so
$\log D=\log(2q)+O(l/q)$.
The lower products instead give
\[
 \frac{s^-_{l,2q-1}+s^-_{l,2q}}{2lq}
             =2\log q+2\log2-4(1+I)+o(1).
\tag{GSR30}
\]
Insert these bounds in GSR8 using GSR16, and then use
$2l/k\to1/2$. The resulting signed conclusion is
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 -1-\log2-2I
 \leq\liminf_{\substack{k\to\infty\\k\equiv1\ (4)}}
                    \frac{\mathfrak T_k}{kq}
 \leq\limsup_{\substack{k\to\infty\\k\equiv1\ (4)}}
                    \frac{\mathfrak T_k}{kq}
 \leq1-\log2+2I.
 \quad}\tag{GSR31}
\]
This is a statement about the actual signed GCR26 value.
The fully finite estimate is GSR23, which is valid also
before any finite JQR cutoff guard holds.

The constants in GSR31 are strictly sharper than those
in GCR39, without relying on a decimal evaluation of $I$.
The counting measure of JQR6 has total mass one and second
moment $2/3$. Its restriction to $|x|\geq a$ has mass $1/2$.
Concavity of $\log$ on this restriction, applied to $x^2$,
therefore gives
\[
 I=\frac12\int_{|x|\geq a}\log x^2\,d\nu(x)
 \leq\frac14\log\left(2\int_{|x|\geq a}x^2\,d\nu(x)\right)
 \leq\frac14\log(4/3).
\tag{GSR32}
\]
This application only averages the explicitly derived spectral
counting law $\nu$; it does not rescale either original Gamma source.
Consequently the upper constant of GSR31 is at most
$1-\tfrac12\log3<1$, and its lower constant is at least
$-1-2\log2+\tfrac12\log3>-1-2\log2$.
GSR25 is the exact value of the sharper constants; no
numerical approximation is needed for these strict improvements.

The original source correction is still
$\Delta_k^\Gamma=\delta_k^\sigma-\mathfrak T_k$ by GCR27.
Using the already proved determinant mass-rate return
$\delta_k^\sigma=o(kq)$ gives
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 -1+\log2-2I
 \leq\liminf_{\substack{k\to\infty\\k\equiv1\ (4)}}
                       \frac{\Delta_k^\Gamma}{kq}
 \leq\limsup_{\substack{k\to\infty\\k\equiv1\ (4)}}
                       \frac{\Delta_k^\Gamma}{kq}
 \leq1+\log2+2I.
 \quad}\tag{GSR33}
\]
The interval in GSR31, like its finite version GSR23,
is an enclosure of the signed value, not its asserted value.
The actual packet weights in GSR18 remain present in GSR15
and GSR20. This proof establishes neither a nonzero leading
value nor a vanishing leading value for that weighted sum;
it retains the sum itself as the complete finite object.


% Independent finite Jacobi/quantile calculation.  No PDF is built here.
\section{A finite Jacobi calculation for the moving upper spectral product}

The matrices and factors in this calculation have their original coefficients.
For an integer \(D\geq1\), let \(J_D\) be the real symmetric matrix on
\(\mathbb R^D\), with coordinates indexed by \(0,\ldots,D-1\), whose diagonal
is zero and whose two entries joining coordinates \(n-1,n\) are
\[
 a_n=\sqrt{n(n-\tfrac12)},\qquad 1\leq n\leq D-1.
 \tag{JQR1}
\]
Put \(A_D=J_D/D\), with the exact inverse relation \(J_D=D A_D\).
This is an auxiliary coordinate for the finite spectrum.  Every power of
\(D\) is retained, in particular in (JQR23); it does not replace the
original source coordinate \(c=k/2\) or the original source measure.
The eigenvalues of \(A_D\), counted with multiplicity, define
\(\mu_D=D^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^D\delta_{\lambda_j(A_D)}\).
For an integer \(l\geq1\), retain every factor in
\[
 t_j=2j+\tfrac12,\qquad
 \Phi_l(y)=\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}(y^2+t_j^2),\qquad
 B_l=\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}t_j^2
     =\frac{l(16l^2-12l-1)}{12}.
 \tag{JQR2}
\]
All eigenvalues of \(\Phi_l(J_D)\) are positive.  They are ordered by the
absolute values of the corresponding eigenvalues of \(J_D\), because
\(\Phi_l(y)\) is strictly increasing as a function of \(|y|\geq0\).
For \(0\leq r\leq D\), write \(\Pi^+_{D,l,r}\) for the product of its
largest \(r\) eigenvalues.  An empty product is one.

\paragraph{The exact finite walk estimate.}
The following bounds hold for every \(D\geq1\) and integer \(p\geq1\):
\[
 \operatorname{spec}(A_D)\subset[-2,2],\qquad
 \frac1D\operatorname{tr}A_D^{\,2p+1}=0,\qquad
 \left|\frac1D\operatorname{tr}A_D^{\,2p}
       -\frac{\binom{2p}{p}}{2p+1}\right|
 \leq \frac{\binom{2p}{p}(2p^2+3p+1)}D .
 \tag{JQR3}
\]
Indeed, \(0<a_n<n\) and every absolute row sum of \(J_D\) is less than
\(2D\), unless the row sum is zero.  The operator norm bound follows
from the maximum absolute row sum for a real symmetric matrix.  The
diagonal matrix with entries \((-1)^i\), \(0\leq i<D\), conjugates
\(J_D\) to \(-J_D\), giving zero trace for every odd power, including
the first power.

Here is the bound for even powers, including the boundary rows.  Set
\(C_p=\binom{2p}{p}\).  There are exactly \(C_p\) sequences of \(2p\)
nearest-neighbour steps with \(p\) positive and \(p\) negative steps.
Starting at a coordinate \(i\) with \(p\leq i\leq D-1-p\), all these
closed walks stay in the matrix.  An edge used by any such walk has
index \(n\) satisfying \(|n-i|\leq p\).  The elementary inequalities
\[
 n-\tfrac12\leq a_n\leq n,\qquad
 \left|\frac{a_n}{D}-\frac{i}{D}\right|
 \leq\frac{p+\tfrac12}{D}
 \tag{JQR4}
\]
follow by squaring the lower inequality and using \(n\geq1\).
Every factor in a walk product and every comparison factor \(i/D\)
belongs to \([0,1]\).  The telescoping identity for a difference of
products therefore bounds the difference between a walk product and
\((i/D)^{2p}\) by \(p(2p+1)/D\).
There are at most \(2p\) remaining, boundary starting coordinates.
At one such coordinate, both the actual sum of valid walk products
and \(C_p(i/D)^{2p}\) lie in \([0,C_p]\); their difference in absolute
value is at most \(C_p\).  This assertion still applies when
\(D\leq2p\), in which case every row may be a boundary row.  Consequently,
\[
 \left|\frac1D\operatorname{tr}A_D^{\,2p}
       -\frac{C_p}{D}\sum_{i=0}^{D-1}(i/D)^{2p}\right|
 \leq\frac{C_p(2p^2+3p)}D.
 \tag{JQR5}
\]
The increasing function \(s^{2p}\) has its left Riemann sum between
its integral minus \(1/D\) and its integral.  Its integral on
\([0,1]\) is \(1/(2p+1)\).  Combining this fact with (JQR5) proves
(JQR3).

\paragraph{The limiting measure and an explicit finite discrepancy.}
Define a probability measure \(\nu\) as the image of
\(ds\,d\theta/\pi\) on \([0,1]\times[0,\pi]\) under
\((s,\theta)\mapsto2s\cos\theta\).  For fixed \(s>0\), its conditional
density is
\(\mathbf1_{\{|x|<2s\}}/(\pi\sqrt{4s^2-x^2})\).
Integrating in \(s\) gives
\[
 d\nu(x)=
 \frac1{2\pi}\operatorname{arcosh}\!\left(\frac2{|x|}\right)
 \mathbf1_{\{0<|x|<2\}}\,dx.
 \tag{JQR6}
\]
There is no atom at zero or at either endpoint.  The integral defining
the probability measure also proves that this logarithmically singular
density is integrable.  Its odd moments vanish, and
\[
 \int x^{2p}\,d\nu(x)=\frac{\binom{2p}{p}}{2p+1},\qquad p\geq0.
 \tag{JQR7}
\]
For completeness, integration in \(s\) supplies \(1/(2p+1)\);
the average of \((2\cos\theta)^{2p}\) on \([0,\pi]\) is
\(\binom{2p}{p}\).  The latter follows by taking the constant coefficient
of \((e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta})^{2p}\) over a full period; the integrand
has period \(\pi\).

For every integer \(L\geq1\), put
\[
 \omega_{D,L}=\frac4{\sqrt L}
               +\frac{2(L+1)^2\,2^L}{D}.
 \tag{JQR8}
\]
For every real Lipschitz function \(f\) on \([-2,2]\), with Lipschitz
constant \(\operatorname{Lip}(f)\), one has the fully finite inequality
\[
 \left|\int f\,d\mu_D-\int f\,d\nu\right|
 \leq \operatorname{Lip}(f)\,\omega_{D,L}.
 \tag{JQR9}
\]
Here is a proof that does not assume a limiting spectral theorem.
Replace \(f\) by \(f-f(0)\), which leaves the difference of integrals
unchanged and gives \(\|f\|_\infty\leq2\operatorname{Lip}(f)\).
Its Bernstein polynomial on \([-2,2]\) is
\[
 P_Lf(x)=\sum_{j=0}^L f(4j/L-2)\binom Lj
       \left(\frac{x+2}{4}\right)^j
       \left(\frac{2-x}{4}\right)^{L-j}.
 \tag{JQR10}
\]
At \(x=4t-2\), this is the expectation of \(f(4X/L-2)\) for
\(X\) binomial with parameters \(L,t\).  Cauchy--Schwarz and
\(\operatorname{Var}(X/L)=t(1-t)/L\) show that
\(\|P_Lf-f\|_\infty\leq2\operatorname{Lip}(f)/\sqrt L\).

Write \(P_Lf=\sum_{h=0}^L b_hx^h\).
For polynomials, the coefficient expression
\(\sum_h|b_h|2^h\) is submultiplicative.  Each of the two linear factors
\((x+2)/4\), \((2-x)/4\) has this expression equal to one.
The binomial expansion in (JQR10) therefore gives
\[
 \sum_{h=0}^L|b_h|2^h\leq2^L\|f\|_\infty.
 \tag{JQR11}
\]
For each nonconstant even \(h=2p\leq L\), (JQR3) bounds the moment
error by \((h+1)^2\,2^h/D\), since
\(\binom{2p}{p}\leq2^{2p}\) and
\(2p^2+3p+1\leq(2p+1)^2\).  The constant and odd errors are zero.
Thus the polynomial integral error is at most
\((L+1)^2\,2^L\|f\|_\infty/D\).
Adding the two Bernstein approximation errors proves (JQR9).
In particular, for \(D\geq4\), the explicit choice
\[
 L_D=\left\lfloor\tfrac12\log_2D\right\rfloor,\qquad
 \omega_D=\omega_{D,L_D}
 \tag{JQR12}
\]
has \(\omega_D\to0\).  For example, its second term is at most
\(2(L_D+1)^2/\sqrt D\).  These estimates apply to the moving dimension
\(D\) itself; no other packet or matrix dimension has been fixed.

\paragraph{The absolute-value distribution and its middle cutoff.}
Let \(F(t)=\nu(\{|x|\leq t\})\).  Extend \(F\) by zero to \(t<0\)
and by one to \(t>2\).  On \([0,2]\) it is
\[
 F(t)=\frac{t\operatorname{arcosh}(2/t)+2\arcsin(t/2)}{\pi},
 \qquad F(0)=0.
 \tag{JQR13}
\]
The formula follows by integrating the density in (JQR6);
differentiation on \((0,2)\) gives
\(F'(t)=\operatorname{arcosh}(2/t)/\pi>0\).
The endpoint values are \(F(0)=0\), \(F(2)=1\), using
\(t\operatorname{arcosh}(2/t)\to0\) at zero.
There is consequently a unique \(a\in(0,2)\) such that
\[
 a\operatorname{arcosh}(2/a)+2\arcsin(a/2)=\frac{\pi}{2}.
 \tag{JQR14}
\]
This number is independent of \(D,l,r\).

Set \(F_D(t)=\mu_D(\{|x|\leq t\})\).  For every \(t\in\mathbb R\)
and every \(\eta>0\), the finite Lipschitz estimate implies
\[
 F(t-\eta)-\frac{\omega_{D,L}}{\eta}
 \leq F_D(t)
 \leq F(t+\eta)+\frac{\omega_{D,L}}{\eta}.
 \tag{JQR15}
\]
To prove the upper bound, majorize the indicator of \(|x|\leq t\)
by a function equal to one up to \(t\), decreasing linearly to zero
between \(t\) and \(t+\eta\), and zero thereafter, as a function of
\(|x|\).  This function has Lipschitz constant at most \(1/\eta\);
its \(\nu\)-integral is at most \(F(t+\eta)\).  The lower bound uses
the corresponding minorant between \(t-\eta\) and \(t\).  When a
cutoff falls outside \([0,2]\), the same clipped ramp argument applies.

For the remaining finite estimates, fix
\[
 c_*=\frac a2,\qquad
 H_{c_*}=\max\{|\log c_*|,\log2\},\qquad
 \theta=\frac rD,\qquad
 I_{\mathrm{top}}=\frac1\pi
       \int_a^2\log x\,\operatorname{arcosh}(2/x)\,dx.
 \tag{JQR16}
\]
Suppose \(1\leq r\leq D\) and that the following explicit finite
inequality holds for some \(L\geq1\):
\[
 F(3a/4)+\frac{4\omega_{D,L}}a<1-\frac rD.
 \tag{JQR17}
\]
Applying (JQR15) with \(t=c_*,\eta=a/4\) proves
\(F_D(c_*)<1-r/D\).  Hence more than \(r\) eigenvalues, counted with
multiplicity, have \(|\lambda_j(A_D)|>c_*\); in particular all the
largest \(r\) absolute values do.  A weak inequality in the counting
statement would suffice, but the strict finite guard (JQR17)
avoids any endpoint convention.

\paragraph{A finite upper-order logarithmic sum.}
Order the absolute eigenvalues as
\(y_{D,1}\geq\cdots\geq y_{D,D}\geq0\).
Under (JQR17),
\[
 \left|\frac1D\sum_{j=1}^r\log y_{D,j}-I_{\mathrm{top}}\right|
 \leq \frac{\omega_{D,L}}{c_*}
       +H_{c_*}\left|\frac rD-\frac12\right|.
 \tag{JQR18}
\]
This estimate follows from an exact variational formula for an ordered
finite sum, which we prove here.  Let
\(g_{c_*}(x)=\log\max(c_*,|x|)\), and, for any probability measure \(\zeta\)
on \([-2,2]\), set
\[
 {\cal Q}_\theta(\zeta)
 =\inf_{u\in[\log c_*,\log2]}
       \left\{\theta u+\int(g_{c_*}(x)-u)_+\,d\zeta(x)\right\}.
 \tag{JQR19}
\]
For \(\zeta=\mu_D\) and \(\theta=r/D\), it equals
\(D^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^r g_{c_*}(y_{D,j})\).
Indeed, at any \(u\) between the \(r\)-th and the \((r+1)\)-st
ordered values of \(g_{c_*}\), the terms above \(u\) give exactly this
sum after addition of \(ru/D\).  At any other \(u\), including ties,
one has
\[
 ru+\sum_{j=1}^D(g_{c_*}(y_{D,j})-u)_+
 \ \geq\ \sum_{j=1}^r g_{c_*}(y_{D,j}),
 \tag{JQR20}
\]
by retaining only the first \(r\) summands and using
\((v-u)_+\geq v-u\).  The endpoint cases \(r=0,D\) follow by
taking \(u=\log2,\log c_*\), respectively.

For every \(u\), the positive-part function in (JQR19) has Lipschitz
constant at most \(1/c_*\).  Equation (JQR9) and the elementary bound
for the difference of two infima therefore give
\[
 |{\cal Q}_\theta(\mu_D)-{\cal Q}_\theta(\nu)|
       \leq\omega_{D,L}/c_*,\qquad
 |{\cal Q}_\theta(\nu)-{\cal Q}_{1/2}(\nu)|
       \leq H_{c_*}|\theta-\tfrac12|.
 \tag{JQR21}
\]
The second bound uses \(|u|\leq H_{c_*}\) throughout the infimum.
Since \(F(a)=1/2\) and \(\nu\) has no atom at \(a\) or \(-a\),
the minimizing cutoff in (JQR19) for \(\nu,\theta=1/2\) is
\(u=\log a\).  To verify it directly, the derivative in \(u\) of
the expression there, wherever needed away from its only possible
atom at \(\log c_*\), is
\(1/2-\nu(g_{c_*}>u)\); its sign changes from negative to positive
at \(\log a\).  Alternatively, integrating (JQR20)'s pointwise
inequality on the set \(|x|\geq a\), of measure \(1/2\), gives the
same minimum without differentiation.  Its value is exactly
\(\int_{|x|\geq a}\log|x|\,d\nu(x)=I_{\mathrm{top}}\).
Under (JQR17), the selected finite values also satisfy
\(g_{c_*}(y_{D,j})=\log y_{D,j}\).  This proves (JQR18).

\paragraph{The complete moving product, with a finite error.}
For every \(D,l,r,L\) in the preceding finite domains satisfying
(JQR17), one has
\[
 \begin{split}
 \left|\log\Pi^+_{D,l,r}
       -2lr\log D-2lD I_{\mathrm{top}}\right|
 &\leq 2lD\left\{\frac{\omega_{D,L}}{c_*}+
           H_{c_*}\left|\frac rD-\frac12\right|\right\}
       +\frac{rB_l}{D^2c_*^2}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{JQR22}
\]
No factor of \(\Phi_l\) has been deleted in this estimate.
For a selected absolute eigenvalue \(y\geq c_*\), its exact contribution
is
\[
 \log\Phi_l(Dy)
 =2l\log D+2l\log y+
    \sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\log\left(1+\frac{t_j^2}{D^2y^2}\right).
 \tag{JQR23}
\]
The final sum is nonnegative and at most \(B_l/(D^2c_*^2)\), by
\(\log(1+s)\leq s\) for \(s\geq0\).  Summing (JQR23) and applying
(JQR18) proves (JQR22).

In particular, along any sequence with
\[
 D\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 l\geq1,\qquad \frac lD\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 1\leq r\leq D,\qquad \frac rD\longrightarrow\frac12,
 \tag{JQR24}
\]
the guard (JQR17) eventually holds with \(L=L_D\), because
\(F(3a/4)<F(a)=1/2\).
Dividing (JQR22) by \(2lD\), its last term becomes
\(\theta B_l/(2lD^2c_*^2)\), which tends to zero since
\(B_l/l=(16l^2-12l-1)/12\).
Thus
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \log\Pi^+_{D,l,r}
     =2lr\log D+2lD I_{\mathrm{top}}+o(lD).}
 \tag{JQR25}
\]
The error conclusion follows from explicit bounds in (JQR22), not
from an asymptotic theorem at fixed \(l\), fixed \(r\), or fixed \(D\).

\paragraph{Application of the domains at the two upper source degrees.}
For the original subsequence \(k=4l+1\), fix an integer \(m\geq1\)
and retain the program's degree
\[
 q=(k+1)^2[1+k(m-1)].
 \tag{JQR26}
\]
For \(N=2q+\varepsilon\), with
\(\varepsilon\in\{-1,0\}\), the matrix dimension and relation rank
in the delegated comparison are exactly
\[
 D=N+l+1=2q+l+\varepsilon+1,\qquad
 r=N+1-q=q+\varepsilon+1,\qquad
 \frac rD-\frac12=\frac{\varepsilon+1-l}{2D}.
 \tag{JQR27}
\]
They satisfy (JQR24), because \(q\geq(k+1)^2\) and
\(l=(k-1)/4\).  Equation (JQR25) therefore applies separately to
both original upper source degrees.  It applies also to the top
\(D-r\) eigenvalues, since their rank fraction tends to \(1/2\).
This statement is about the given finite matrix spectrum.  The map
from the original relation subspace into this matrix and the determinant
comparison for that subspace must be retained in its surrounding
calculation; no subspace identification is being inferred from a
matching rank.

\paragraph{An exact full-product calculation and the lower product.}
There is also an elementary finite identity for the full determinant
that avoids a logarithm at a zero eigenvalue of \(J_D\).
Let \(b_n(y)\) be the monic polynomials defined by
\[
 b_0(y)=1,\quad b_1(y)=y,\quad
 b_{n+1}(y)=y b_n(y)-n(n-\tfrac12)b_{n-1}(y)\quad(n\geq1).
 \tag{JQR28}
\]
Expansion of the tridiagonal determinant along its last row proves
that \(b_D(y)=\det(yI_D-J_D)\).  As a formal power series in \(z\),
the recurrence implies
\[
 B(y,z)=\sum_{n\geq0}\frac{b_n(y)}{n!}z^n
  =(1+iz)^{-1/4-iy/2}(1-iz)^{-1/4+iy/2}.
 \tag{JQR29}
\]
To check every sign, multiply the recurrence by \(z^n/n!\) and
sum.  One obtains
\((1+z^2)\partial_z B=(y-z/2)B\), with \(B(y,0)=1\).
The right side of (JQR29) solves this equation with this initial
coefficient, so equality follows recursively for every coefficient.
All powers here are their formal series with constant term one.

At the original factor \(y=it_j=i(2j+1/2)\), this gives exactly
\[
 B(it_j,z)=(1+iz)^j(1-iz)^{-j-1/2},\qquad
 b_D(it_j)=i^D D!\,A_{D,j},
 \quad
 A_{D,j}=\sum_{s=0}^{\min(j,D)}
   \binom js\frac{(j+\tfrac12)_{D-s}}{(D-s)!}.
 \tag{JQR30}
\]
Here \((u)_n=u(u+1)\cdots(u+n-1)\), with \((u)_0=1\).
Every coefficient in \(A_{D,j}\) is positive, and the following
finite bounds hold for \(D\geq1\), \(0\leq j<l\):
\[
 \frac1{2D+1}\leq A_{D,j}\leq[2(D+l)]^j.
 \tag{JQR31}
\]
For the lower bound, retain the \(s=0\) term and use
\[
 \frac{(j+\tfrac12)_D}{D!}\geq
 \frac{(\tfrac12)_D}{D!}
 =4^{-D}\binom{2D}{D}\geq\frac1{2D+1}.
 \tag{JQR32}
\]
The last inequality follows because the central binomial coefficient
is the largest of the \(2D+1\) nonnegative coefficients summing to
\(4^D\); the ratio of consecutive coefficients verifies maximality.
For the upper bound, for \(0\leq n\leq D\),
\[
 \frac{(j+\tfrac12)_n}{n!}
 \leq\frac{(j+1)_n}{n!}=\binom{n+j}{j}
 \leq(D+j)^j.
 \tag{JQR33}
\]
The last inequality holds also at \(j=0\), when both sides are one;
for \(j\geq1\), write the binomial coefficient as a product of \(j\)
factors at most \(D+j\), divided by \(j!\geq1\).
Summing \(\binom js\) over the actual truncated range gives at most
\(2^j\), proving (JQR31).

Since \(J_D\) has real eigenvalues,
\(\det(J_D^2+t_j^2I_D)=|b_D(it_j)|^2\).
Equations (JQR30)--(JQR31), with all \(l\) factors present, now give
\[
 \begin{split}
 \log\det\Phi_l(J_D)
   &=2l\log(D!)+2\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\log A_{D,j},\\
 2l\log(D!)-2l\log(2D+1)
   &\leq\log\det\Phi_l(J_D)\\
   &\leq2l\log(D!)+l(l-1)\log[2(D+l)].
 \end{split}
 \tag{JQR34}
\]
For explicit finite remainders, monotonicity of \(\log x\) gives
\[
 D\log D-D+1\leq\log(D!)
       \leq D\log D-D+1+\log D.
 \tag{JQR35}
\]
Consequently the full determinant differs from
\(2lD(\log D-1)\) by a quantity between
\(2l-2l\log(2D+1)\) and
\(2l(1+\log D)+l(l-1)\log[2(D+l)]\).
In addition to (JQR24), the sufficient condition for these full
remainders to be \(o(lD)\) is
\[
 \frac{l\log[2(D+l)]}{D}\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{JQR36}
\]
This stronger condition holds at each of the actual dimensions
(JQR27), where \(D\geq2(k+1)^2\) and \(l=(k-1)/4\).
It is not silently replaced by the weaker condition \(l/D\to0\).

Let \(\Pi^-_{D,l,r}\) denote the product of the smallest \(r\)
eigenvalues of \(\Phi_l(J_D)\).
The exact identity
\(\Pi^-_{D,l,r}\Pi^+_{D,l,D-r}=\det\Phi_l(J_D)\),
combined with (JQR25), (JQR34)--(JQR36), proves at the actual
dimensions, and more generally under (JQR24) and (JQR36),
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \log\Pi^-_{D,l,r}
   =2lr\log D-2lD(1+I_{\mathrm{top}})+o(lD).}
 \tag{JQR37}
\]
This deduction retains possible zero eigenvalues of \(J_D\) inside
the strictly positive factors \(J_D^2+t_j^2I_D\).
For the completely finite version, assume the guard (JQR17) with
rank \(D-r\), and define
\[
 E^-_{D,l,r,L}
 =2lD\left\{\frac{\omega_{D,L}}{c_*}+
           H_{c_*}\left|\frac rD-\frac12\right|\right\}
       +\frac{(D-r)B_l}{D^2c_*^2}.
 \tag{JQR38}
\]
Subtracting the upper-rank estimate (JQR22), with rank \(D-r\),
from the two finite bounds following (JQR35) gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 2l-2l\log(2D+1)-E^-_{D,l,r,L}
 &\leq
 \log\Pi^-_{D,l,r}
    -\{2lr\log D-2lD(1+I_{\mathrm{top}})\}\\
 &\leq
 2l(1+\log D)+l(l-1)\log[2(D+l)]+E^-_{D,l,r,L}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{JQR39}
\]
Thus both upper and lower spectral products have explicit finite
enclosures.  No lower-tail logarithmic approximation is needed.

% END EXACT REQUIRED PROOF GSR_JQR

\subsection*{Retained proof provider: QDC}

\section{Exact source determinant deficit on restrictions and quotient metrics}

All coefficient spaces in this calculation are complex finite-dimensional
spaces, and a star denotes conjugate transpose in the stated coefficient
frames. A positive matrix is Hermitian positive definite. Determinants on
the zero-dimensional space are one, and its inverse is the unique empty
matrix. These conventions include zero boundary rank and zero kernel rank.
The maps below preserve the original coefficient vectors; their displayed
isometries calculate the comparison of two forms on those same vectors.

\subsection{The minimum section and the exact determinant factorization}

Let $D$ be a positive $n$ by $n$ matrix and let $A>0$ satisfy
$D\preceq A1_n$. Let
$L:\mathbb C^n\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb C^r$ be a fixed surjective
matrix, with $0\le r\le n$. Set
\[
 Q_D=(LD^{-1}L^*)^{-1},\qquad Q_1=(LL^*)^{-1},\qquad
 S_D=D^{-1}L^*Q_D,\qquad \mathsf E=A^{-1}D .
 \tag{QDC1}
\]
For every nonzero $z\in\mathbb C^r$, $L^*z\ne0$, whence
$z^*LD^{-1}L^*z>0$. Thus the displayed inverses exist.
Direct multiplication gives
\[
 LS_D=1_r,\qquad S_D^*DS_D=Q_D,\qquad
 z^*DS_D=0\quad(z\in\ker L).
 \tag{QDC2}
\]
Every lift of $y\in\mathbb C^r$ is uniquely of the form $S_Dy+z$
with $z\in\ker L$, and its exact norm is
\[
 (S_Dy+z)^*D(S_Dy+z)=y^*Q_Dy+z^*Dz.
 \tag{QDC3}
\]
Consequently $S_D$ is the unique minimum section of $L$ for the
form $D$, with the original target coefficient frame retained.

Define the Euclidean isometry and choose its orthogonal complement
by
\[
 W=L^*Q_1^{1/2}:\mathbb C^r\longrightarrow\mathbb C^n,\qquad
 K:\mathbb C^{n-r}\longrightarrow\ker L,\qquad
 W^*W=1_r,\quad K^*K=1_{n-r},\quad K^*W=0.
 \tag{QDC4}
\]
Such a $K$ is obtained by choosing any orthonormal basis of $\ker L$.
The matrix $[K,W]$ is unitary: its orthogonal summands have dimensions
$n-r$ and $r$. Also
$L=Q_1^{-1/2}W^*$ and $LW=Q_1^{-1/2}$.
In this full source frame put
\[
 F=K^*\mathsf EK,\qquad B=K^*\mathsf EW,\qquad
 C=W^*\mathsf EW,\qquad T=C-B^*F^{-1}B.
 \tag{QDC5}
\]
Positivity of $\mathsf E$ gives positivity of $F$. For $y\ne0$,
the vector $W y-KF^{-1}By$ is nonzero since its $W$ component is
$y$. Its $\mathsf E$ norm squared is $y^*Ty$, proving $T>0$.
The block triangular matrix
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1_{n-r}&-F^{-1}B\\0&1_r\end{pmatrix}
\]
has determinant one. Congruence by this matrix transforms
$[K,W]^*\mathsf E[K,W]$ into $\operatorname{diag}(F,T)$.
This proves the exact determinant identity
\[
 \det\mathsf E=\det F\det T .
 \tag{QDC6}
\]
The lower right block of the inverse of the same block matrix is
$T^{-1}$, as follows either by multiplying the two triangular
factors and the diagonal inverse, or by solving
$Fz+By=0$ and $B^*z+Cy=v$. Thus
\[
 T=(W^*\mathsf E^{-1}W)^{-1},\qquad
 Q_D=AQ_1^{1/2}TQ_1^{1/2},\qquad
 S_DQ_1^{-1/2}=W-KF^{-1}B .
 \tag{QDC7}
\]
For the section equality the right side has the required $L$ image
$Q_1^{-1/2}$, and its $\mathsf E$ inner product with every vector
in $\ker L$ is zero by $F(-F^{-1}B)+B=0$. The uniqueness proved
in QDC3 gives the equality; scaling a source form by $A$ does not
alter its minimum section.

The inequality $0<\mathsf E\preceq1_n$ implies
$0<F\preceq1_{n-r}$ and $0<C\preceq1_r$. The matrix
$B^*F^{-1}B$ is positive semidefinite, so
$0<T\preceq C\preceq1_r$. All eigenvalues of $F$ and of $T$
therefore belong to $(0,1]$. Their determinants, being the products
of these eigenvalues, belong to $(0,1]$. QDC6--7 prove
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \det\mathsf E
 \ \le\ \frac{\det Q_D}{A^r\det Q_1}
 \ =\ \det T\ \le\ 1 .}
 \tag{QDC8}
\]
There is no assertion here that a lower bound on a full source
condition number persists after the quotient. QDC8 follows from
the complete determinant and the literal minimum-section map.

The full determinant deficit is the nonnegative scalar
\[
 d(D,A)=n\log A-\log\det D=-\log\det\mathsf E.
 \tag{QDC9}
\]
Its exact decomposition, including the retained kernel, is
\[
 \begin{split}
 d(D,A)&=d_{\ker L}+d_{\mathrm{quot}},\\
 d_{\ker L}&=-\log\det F\ge0,\\
 d_{\mathrm{quot}}
 &=r\log A+\log\det Q_1-\log\det Q_D
   =-\log\det T\ge0.
 \end{split}
 \tag{QDC10}
\]
In particular $d_{\mathrm{quot}}\le d(D,A)$.
For $r=0$, $F=\mathsf E$ in a unitary frame and $T$ is empty.
For $r=n$, $F$ is empty and $T$ is a unitary conjugate of
$\mathsf E$. The preceding formulas remain literal in both cases.

\subsection{Restrictions and original source frames}

Let $Y:\mathbb C^s\hookrightarrow\mathbb C^n$ be any fixed injective
matrix. Its Euclidean Gram $Y^*Y$ is positive. Define
$V=Y(Y^*Y)^{-1/2}$, so that $V^*V=1_s$. Completing $V$ to a
unitary matrix $[V,Z]$ and repeating the preceding triangular
calculation, now with $V^*\mathsf EV$ as the first block, gives
\[
 \det\mathsf E=\det(V^*\mathsf EV)\det
 \left(Z^*\mathsf EZ
       -Z^*\mathsf EV(V^*\mathsf EV)^{-1}V^*\mathsf EZ\right).
 \tag{QDC11}
\]
The second factor is the determinant of a positive contraction:
its positivity is the squared norm of the corresponding
orthogonal minimum vector, and it is at most $Z^*\mathsf EZ\preceq1$.
The first factor is also the determinant of a positive contraction.
Therefore
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \det\mathsf E\le
 \frac{\det(Y^*DY)}{A^s\det(Y^*Y)}
 =\det(V^*\mathsf EV)\le1 .}
 \tag{QDC12}
\]
This proves the restriction statement for its original domain
frame, including $s=0$ and $s=n$.

For use with the original polynomial source, let $H_0,H$ be
positive matrices on the same original coefficient space
$\mathcal V=\mathbb C^n$, with $H\preceq AH_0$. Retain a fixed
onto matrix $J:\mathcal V\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal E$ and set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 D=H_0^{-1/2}HH_0^{-1/2},\qquad L=JH_0^{-1/2},\\
 G_0=(JH_0^{-1}J^*)^{-1},\quad
 G=(JH^{-1}J^*)^{-1},\quad
 R_H=H^{-1}J^*G.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{QDC13}
\]
The coordinate map $b=H_0^{1/2}a$ preserves both original
forms exactly:
\[
 a^*H_0a=b^*b,\qquad a^*Ha=b^*Db,\qquad Ja=Lb.
 \tag{QDC14}
\]
Thus $Q_D=G$, $Q_1=G_0$, and $R_H=H_0^{-1/2}S_D$.
The maps in QDC13--14 prove the claimed correspondence between
the original source and QDC1; no source vector, relation or
target coefficient is discarded. In these original frames,
QDC8 becomes
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 d(H,H_0;A)=n\log A-\log\frac{\det H}{\det H_0}\ge0,\\
 0\le
 r\log A-\log\frac{\det G}{\det G_0}
 \le d(H,H_0;A).
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{QDC15}
\]
Here $r=\dim\mathcal E$. For an original injection
$Y_0:\mathbb C^s\hookrightarrow\mathcal V$, the map in QDC12
is $Y=H_0^{1/2}Y_0$. It proves
\[
 0\le s\log A
       -\log\frac{\det(Y_0^*HY_0)}{\det(Y_0^*H_0Y_0)}
 \le d(H,H_0;A).
 \tag{QDC16}
\]
If $Y_0$ spans exactly $\ker J$, its restriction deficit and
the quotient deficit in QDC15 sum exactly to $d(H,H_0;A)$.
Indeed $H_0^{1/2}Y_0(Y_0^*H_0Y_0)^{-1/2}$ is an orthonormal
basis of $\ker L$; substituting this $K$ into QDC10 proves
the statement with every original determinant retained.

\subsection{The four original polynomial quotients}

Retain the original packet, its integer multiplicity $m\ge1$,
and its parameters $\delta,\gamma$ from the full quartet.
Write
\[
 \ell_k=1+k(m-1),\quad q_k=(k+1)^2\ell_k,\quad c_k=k/2,\quad
 \chi_k(S)=\prod_{a,b=0}^k
 \bigl(S-c_k-(2a-k)\delta-i(2b-k)\gamma\bigr)^{\ell_k}.
 \tag{QDC17}
\]
The coefficient space is
$\mathcal E_k=\mathbb C[S]/(\chi_k)$, in its increasing
remainder frame of degrees zero through $q_k-1$.
The source $\mathcal P_N$ retains its increasing power frame
$1,S,\ldots,S^N$ and its literal value $S=c_k+iy$.
For $N\ge q_k-1$ its onto map is the unchanged monic remainder
\[
 J_{k,N}:\mathcal P_N\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal E_k,\qquad
 J_{k,N}P=[P],\qquad
 \ker J_{k,N}=\chi_k\mathcal P_{N-q_k}.
 \tag{QDC18}
\]
The last space is zero when $N=q_k-1$. The full-unit inclusion
$[P]\mapsto U^{\otimes k}[P(s_1+\cdots+s_k)]$ remains the
original subsequent map; QDC18 makes no replacement of $U$,
of its jets, or of the tensor nilpotents.

Let $w_h(y)=|v_h(1/2+iy)|^2/(2\pi)$ be the original
arithmetic source density, with the entire quotient $v_h=g/h$
and $g=2\xi$ from that packet. Put $\alpha=\pi/2$ and
$M_\alpha=\int_{\mathbb R}e^{\alpha y}w_h(y)\,dy>0$.
The two forms used in this calculation are exactly
\[
 \begin{split}
 (H_{k,N})_{ab}
 &=\int_{\mathbb R}\overline{(c_k+iy)}^{\,a}(c_k+iy)^b
                    w_h^{*k}(y)\,dy,\\
 (H_{0,k,N})_{ab}
 &=\int_{\mathbb R}\overline{(c_k+iy)}^{\,a}(c_k+iy)^b
       \frac{|\Gamma(1/4+iy/2)|^2}{2\pi}\,dy ,
 \qquad 0\le a,b\le N .
 \end{split}
 \tag{QDC19}
\]
Thus $H_{0,k,N}=H_N^{\sigma,k}$ is the fixed one-factor Gamma
measure evaluated on the same shifted powers. Its total mass
is $\sqrt{2\pi}$. It is not the $k$-fold Gamma convolution.
The total mass of the first measure is the original $\mu_h^k$.
All moments and the strict positivity of these forms are
those of the original source calculation.

The full source upper comparison has the same scalar at every
degree:
\[
 H_{k,N}\preceq A_kH_{0,k,N},\qquad
 A_k=\frac{C_h}{c_\Gamma}\,k^{p+1}M_\alpha^{\,k-1},
 \quad p=8m-\frac92,\quad c_\Gamma=\sqrt2\,e^{-7/6}.
 \tag{QDC20}
\]
Here $C_h$ is the retained original tilt constant; none of these
constants are absorbed into the measures or coefficient frames.
For these matrices let $D_{k,N}$ be QDC13's exact relative
operator and let
\[
 \begin{split}
 G_{k,N}&=(J_{k,N}H_{k,N}^{-1}J_{k,N}^*)^{-1},\\
 G^\sigma_{k,N}
   &=(J_{k,N}H_{0,k,N}^{-1}J_{k,N}^*)^{-1},\\
 d_{k,N}&=(N+1)\log A_k-\log\det D_{k,N},\\
 b_{k,N}&=q_k\log A_k
                -\log\frac{\det G_{k,N}}{\det G^\sigma_{k,N}}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{QDC21}
\]
The exact map $L_{k,N}=J_{k,N}H_{0,k,N}^{-1/2}$ in
QDC13 proves, separately at each degree and without any
degree-dependent choice of quotient,
\[
 0\le b_{k,N}\le d_{k,N}.
 \tag{QDC22}
\]

For the four specified endpoints, retain their signs and
their source dimensions:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 (N_0,N_1,N_2,N_3)=(q_k-1,q_k,2q_k-1,2q_k),\\
 (\varepsilon_0,\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,\varepsilon_3)
      =(1,1,-1,-1),\\
 (N_0+1,N_1+1,N_2+1,N_3+1)
      =(q_k,q_k+1,2q_k,2q_k+1).
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{QDC23}
\]
Define their original quotient determinant sums
$F_{\rm ar,k}=\sum_i\varepsilon_i\log\det G_{k,N_i}$
and
$F_{\sigma,k}=\sum_i\varepsilon_i\log\det G^\sigma_{k,N_i}$.
Substituting the exact QDC21 equality, not replacing any
of its four deficits by a common unknown error, gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 F_{\rm ar,k}-F_{\sigma,k}
 &=-b_{k,q_k-1}-b_{k,q_k}
                  +b_{k,2q_k-1}+b_{k,2q_k},\\
 -d_{k,q_k-1}-d_{k,q_k}
 &\le F_{\rm ar,k}-F_{\sigma,k}
 \le d_{k,2q_k-1}+d_{k,2q_k}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{QDC24}
\]
The scalar $q_k\log A_k$ cancels because every quotient
has the same dimension $q_k$ and the stated signs sum to zero.
The four source dimensions differ and were retained in
$d_{k,N}$; their scalar factors were not canceled incorrectly.

Here is the exact passage from a source determinant rate to
this signed quantity. Define the literal source-rate remainder
and the literal upper-rate remainder by
\[
 e_{k,N}=\frac{\log\det D_{k,N}}{k(N+1)}-\log M_\alpha,
 \qquad
 \epsilon_k^A=\frac{(p+1)\log k+\log(C_h/c_\Gamma)-\log M_\alpha}{k}.
 \tag{QDC25}
\]
These are definitions, and their relation to the finite
deficit is the identity
\[
 \frac{d_{k,N}}{kq_k}
   =\frac{N+1}{q_k}(\epsilon_k^A-e_{k,N})\ge0.
 \tag{QDC26}
\]
In particular $\epsilon_k^A\to0$. The source determinant asymptotic proved in DMR26,
$\log\det D_{k,N_i}=(N_i+1)k\log M_\alpha+o(k(N_i+1))$
is precisely $e_{k,N_i}\to0$ at each of these four endpoints.
Its use in QDC26 is exact: the dimension multipliers in
QDC23 are at most $2+1/q_k$, so all four
$d_{k,N_i}/(kq_k)$ tend to zero. Substituting this result
into the two sides of QDC24 proves the requested propagation
\[
 \frac{F_{\rm ar,k}-F_{\sigma,k}}{kq_k}\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{QDC27}
\]
The finite inequality QDC24 is independent of any asymptotic.
QDC25--27 specify exactly how the full determinant estimate,
proved in the source determinant calculation, enters the
quotient calculation. This independent calculation does not
replace that estimate by an assumption about quotient
eigenvalues, a fixed-degree limit, or four diagonal entries.

\subsection{The actual boundary and its retained kernel}

Fix the original onto observation
$\Lambda_k:\mathcal E_k\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal B_k$,
with its actual image as codomain, and its fixed kernel
inclusion $I_k:\mathcal K_k\hookrightarrow\mathcal E_k$.
This includes the period and monodromy observation with the
entire $U^{\otimes k}$ factor already present. Put
$r_k=\dim\mathcal B_k$ and $t_k=q_k-r_k$.
For either of the two quotient Grams $G$ in QDC21 define
\[
 H^K(G)=I_k^*GI_k,\quad
 Q^B(G)=(\Lambda_kG^{-1}\Lambda_k^*)^{-1},\quad
 S^B(G)=G^{-1}\Lambda_k^*Q^B(G).
 \tag{QDC28}
\]
The exact section identities QDC2--3 apply to $\Lambda_k$:
its section is $G$-orthogonal to $I_k$, and every vector
of $\mathcal E_k$ decomposes uniquely into a kernel vector
and this section's lift. In particular
$[I_k,S^B(G)]^*G[I_k,S^B(G)]
 =\operatorname{diag}(H^K(G),Q^B(G))$.

Choose once a coefficient right inverse $S_*$ of $\Lambda_k$,
independent of $N$ and of which source form is used.
The difference $S^B(G)-S_*$ has image in $\mathcal K_k$,
so is uniquely $I_kT_G$ for a matrix $T_G$. Therefore
\[
 \det[I_k,S^B(G)]=\det[I_k,S_*],\qquad
 \det H^K(G)\det Q^B(G)
        =|\det[I_k,S_*]|^2\det G.
 \tag{QDC29}
\]
This proves the exact frame cancellation in a ratio of the
two sources as well as in the four endpoint sum.

Minimizing $H_{k,N}\preceq A_kH_{0,k,N}$ on each identical
fibre of $J_{k,N}$ proves
$G_{k,N}\preceq A_kG^\sigma_{k,N}$ directly from QDC3.
Use this pair of forms in QDC15--16 with the fixed
$\Lambda_k$ and $I_k$. Define
\[
 \begin{split}
 b^K_{k,N}
 &=t_k\log A_k-
      \log\frac{\det H^K(G_{k,N})}
                    {\det H^K(G^\sigma_{k,N})},\\
 b^B_{k,N}
 &=r_k\log A_k-
      \log\frac{\det Q^B(G_{k,N})}
                    {\det Q^B(G^\sigma_{k,N})}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{QDC30}
\]
Both are nonnegative by the exact restriction and quotient
inequalities. QDC29 proves their exact sum, and QDC22 bounds it:
\[
 b^K_{k,N}+b^B_{k,N}=b_{k,N}\le d_{k,N}.
 \tag{QDC31}
\]
For either superscript $X=K$ or $X=B$, its arithmetic-minus-
reference four-endpoint determinant sum is consequently
\[
 \Delta F^X_k
   =-b^X_{k,q_k-1}-b^X_{k,q_k}
                   +b^X_{k,2q_k-1}+b^X_{k,2q_k},
 \qquad
 -d_{k,q_k-1}-d_{k,q_k}\le\Delta F^X_k
      \le d_{k,2q_k-1}+d_{k,2q_k}.
 \tag{QDC32}
\]
Its common scalar cancels with the same four signs, with
dimension $t_k$ or $r_k$, respectively. The original complete
sum satisfies the exact equality
$F_{\rm ar,k}-F_{\sigma,k}=\Delta F^K_k+\Delta F^B_k$.
By the original-source determinant limit DMR26 and the exact propagation QDC25--27,
each of $\Delta F^K_k/(kq_k)$ and
$\Delta F^B_k/(kq_k)$ tends to zero separately.
Neither the kernel nor a mixed block in its minimum section
has been deleted.

These identities control the rate relative to the specified
fixed one-factor Gamma source. They do not assign a sign to
QDC24 or QDC32, identify that reference with the original
$k$-fold Gamma endpoint, or prove an RH contradiction.
In particular, $o(kq_k)$ is the scale established by
QDC25--27; a smaller scale does not follow by renaming
the four deficits.

\section{The exact closed equilibrium coefficient}
\label{sec:closed-equilibrium-intake}

This section retains the potential, measure, and energy of EIQ1--33:
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL1}
\begin{gathered}
V_{\alpha,\beta}(x)=\beta\sqrt{x}-\alpha\log x,\qquad
\alpha>0,\quad\beta>0,\quad x>0,\\
\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(dx)=\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(x)\,dx,\qquad
F(\alpha,\beta)=
\iint\log|x-y|\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(x)d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(y)
-\int V_{\alpha,\beta}\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta},\\
\mathcal L(\alpha,\beta)=\int\log x\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(x).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The preceding complete EIQ proof supplies the unique minimizing
probability measure, its positive density and exact support
$[a,b]=[u^2,v^2]$, and its Euler equality. In particular, no assertion
in this section changes the minimization problem or chooses another
measure. The endpoints are exactly
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL2}
uK(k)=\frac{\alpha\pi}{\beta},\qquad
vE(k)=\frac{(\alpha+2)\pi}{\beta},\qquad
k^2=1-\frac{u^2}{v^2},\qquad 0<u<v.
\end{equation}
Here $K$ and $E$ have the modulus convention of EIQ2, not the
elliptic-parameter convention. Define the original endpoint quantities
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL3}
w=\frac{u+v}{2},\qquad z=uv,\qquad
c_{\mathrm{cap}}=\frac{v^2-u^2}{4},\qquad
d\nu(x)=\frac{dx}{\pi\sqrt{(b-x)(x-a)}}\quad(a<x<b).
\end{equation}
The letter $z$ in ECL3 is a positive scalar. A Cauchy-transform variable
below is denoted by $\lambda$. The measure $\nu$ has mass one by
$x=(a+b)/2+(b-a)\cos\theta/2$, $0\leq\theta\leq\pi$.

\subsection{Arcsine identities with their original constants}

Put $m=(a+b)/2$, $d=(b-a)/2$, and $\eta=(v-u)/(v+u)$.
Then $0<\eta<1$ and
\[
x=m+d\cos\theta
 =w^2(1+2\eta\cos\theta+\eta^2),\qquad
z=w^2(1-\eta^2).
\]
The absolutely uniformly convergent series
\[
\begin{split}
\log x&=2\log w+
  2\sum_{n\geq1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}\eta^n}{n}\cos(n\theta),\\
\frac1x&=\frac1z\left(1+
  2\sum_{n\geq1}(-\eta)^n\cos(n\theta)\right)
\end{split}
\]
follow from the geometric series and the integral of that series.
Termwise integration, and orthogonality of the cosines, prove
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL4}
\begin{split}
\int\log x\,d\nu(x)&=2\log w,\qquad
\int\frac{d\nu(x)}x=\frac1z,\\
\int\frac{\log x}{x}\,d\nu(x)
 &=\frac{2\log w+2\log(1-\eta^2)}z
 =\frac{2\log z-2\log w}z.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
In the product of the two Fourier series, the contribution of degree
$n\geq1$ to the constant coefficient is
$-2\eta^{2n}/(nz)$, giving the displayed logarithm without an omitted
constant.

The logarithmic potential of $\nu$ on its entire support is
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL5}
\int_a^b\log|x-t|\,d\nu(x)=\log c_{\mathrm{cap}},
\qquad a\leq t\leq b.
\end{equation}
Here is a direct proof, including the endpoint values. Write
$t=m+d\cos\phi$ and use
\[
|m+d\cos\theta-t|
=\frac d2
 |e^{i\theta}-e^{i\phi}|\,
 |e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\phi}|.
\]
The integrand depending on $\cos\theta$ has the same mean on
$[0,\pi]$ as on $[0,2\pi]$. For $0<r<1$, the power series of
$\log|1-re^{i\theta}|$ has zero circle mean. This mean tends to
the mean at $r=1$: near its only singular angle,
$|1-re^{i\theta}|^2=(1-r)^2+2r(1-\cos\theta)$,
so for $r\geq1/2$ the absolute value of its negative logarithm
is bounded by $C+|\log|\theta||$, and its positive logarithm
is bounded by $\log2$. These are integrable bounds. A rotation
proves that each of the two circle means above is zero, for
every $\phi$, including $\phi=0,\pi$. Hence their sum is
$\log(d/2)=\log c_{\mathrm{cap}}$, proving ECL5.

All logarithmic double integrals used below are absolutely integrable.
In fact the absolute logarithm for two arcsine variables is bounded by
a constant plus
\[
\left|\log\left|\sin\frac{\theta-\phi}{2}\right|\right|
+\left|\log\left|\sin\frac{\theta+\phi}{2}\right|\right|.
\]
Each term is integrable on $[0,\pi]^2$; near a zero, use
$\sin t\geq 2t/\pi$ for $0\leq t\leq\pi/2$, and integrate
$|\log t|$. Thus the same assertion holds for any two signed
measures whose absolute values are bounded by constant multiples
of $\nu$. The EIQ density also has this domination, because
$\rho(x)/[1/(\pi\sqrt{(b-x)(x-a)})]$ is bounded on $[a,b]$.

Let $\ell$ have the exact EIQ17 convention
\[
V_{\alpha,\beta}(x)
-2\int\log|x-t|\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(t)=\ell
\quad(a\leq x\leq b).
\]
Integrating this equality against $\nu$, using ECL4--5 and
EIQ7, gives
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL6}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\ell=2(\alpha+2)-2\alpha\log w-2\log c_{\mathrm{cap}}.}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\int\sqrt{x}\,d\nu(x)=2vE(k)/\pi$, whose product with
$\beta$ is $2(\alpha+2)$ by ECL2. Fubini is justified by the
absolute integrability just proved and the unit mass of $\mu$.
Smoothness of $\ell$ now follows from the endpoint smoothness in EIQ6.

\subsection{Differentiation with the moving endpoints retained}

All derivatives in this subsection are with respect to $\alpha$ at
fixed $\beta$. Put
\[
p_\alpha(x)=\frac{h_{\alpha,\beta}(x)}{2\pi},\qquad
D(x)=(b-x)(x-a).
\]
In this subsection a dot denotes a parameter derivative, so that
$\dot p(x)$ differentiates the function $p_\alpha(x)$ at fixed $x$.
The function $h_{\alpha,\beta}$ is exactly EIQ10; the subscript on
$p_\alpha$ labels the parameter and does not denote differentiation.
EIQ10 and EIQ29 prove that $p_\alpha$ and its parameter and spatial
derivatives are bounded on a fixed positive compact neighbourhood
of the supports when parameters range in a sufficiently small
compact neighbourhood.

The derivative of the probability measure, interpreted by integration
against every continuously differentiable function on this neighbourhood,
is the finite signed measure
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL7}
\begin{split}
\sigma(dx)&=\left[
\dot p(x)\sqrt{D(x)}
+\frac{p_\alpha(x)}{2\sqrt{D(x)}}
 \{\dot b(x-a)-\dot a(b-x)\}\right]\mathbf1_{(a,b)}(x)\,dx,\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\int f\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}
&=\int f\,d\sigma,\qquad \sigma([a,b])=0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
This identity includes the moving support. To prove it without
postulating endpoint terms, set $\delta=b-a$ and $x=a+\delta y$.
The measure in the fixed variable $y$ is
\[
\delta^2 p_\alpha(a+\delta y)\sqrt{y(1-y)}\,dy.
\]
Its differentiated integral against $f$ is
\[
\int_0^1\sqrt{y(1-y)}
\left[
2\delta\dot\delta\,p_\alpha f
+\delta^2(\dot p+p_\alpha'\dot x)f
+\delta^2p_\alpha f'\dot x
\right]dy,\qquad \dot x=\dot a+\dot\delta y.
\]
Replace $\delta f'$ in the last summand by $d f(x)/dy$ and
integrate by parts. Its boundary term is
$[\delta p_\alpha(x)\dot x\sqrt{y(1-y)}f(x)]_0^1=0$.
The remaining terms combine, on returning to $x$, to
$\dot p\sqrt D+p_\alpha\dot D/(2\sqrt D)$, where
$\dot D=\dot b(x-a)-\dot a(b-x)$, exactly ECL7.
This integration by parts is justified first on
$[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]$ and then by passage to the limit:
the boundary expression tends to zero and the differentiated
square-root factor is bounded by a constant times
$[y(1-y)]^{-1/2}$, an integrable function. The differentiated
fixed-interval integral is valid by the uniform bounds above.
Taking $f=1$ proves the zero total mass in ECL7.
The displayed density also proves $|\sigma|\leq C\nu$.
In particular no endpoint atom has appeared or been discarded.

For fixed $x\in(a,b)$, differentiating the Euler equality gives
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL8}
-\log x-2\int\log|x-t|\,d\sigma(t)=\dot\ell.
\end{equation}
For precision, choose a smooth function of $t$ equal to one near
$x$, with support in a common interior subinterval for nearby
parameters. On this subinterval the density and its parameter
derivative are uniformly bounded, so differentiation of its
logarithmic integral follows by domination by
$C|\log|x-t||$. The complementary integrand is a continuously
differentiable test function, so ECL7 applies. This proves ECL8
despite the logarithmic singularity and the moving endpoints.

We next identify $\sigma$ exactly:
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL9}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\mu_{\alpha,\beta}(dx)
=\sigma(dx)=\frac12\left(1-\frac z x\right)d\nu(x).}
\end{equation}
The derivative has the test-function meaning proved in ECL7,
which in particular applies to the logarithmic and square-root
moments because $a>0$.

Here is an explicit uniqueness argument establishing ECL9.
Denote its proposed right side by $\sigma_0$.
By ECL4 it has zero total mass, and $|\sigma_0|\leq C\nu$.
Retain the branch
$R(\lambda)=\sqrt{(\lambda-a)(\lambda-b)}$ with
$R(\lambda)/\lambda\to1$ at infinity, exactly as in EIQ12.
The arcsine transform proved there and partial fractions yield
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL10}
\begin{split}
\int\frac{d\sigma_0(t)}{\lambda-t}
&=\frac1{2R(\lambda)}
-\frac z{2\lambda}
 \left(\frac1z+\frac1{R(\lambda)}\right)\\
&=\frac{1-z/\lambda}{2R(\lambda)}-\frac1{2\lambda}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
There is no pole at zero: $R(0)=-uv=-z$, so the numerator
$(\lambda-z)/R(\lambda)-1$ vanishes there. The integral
itself is analytic in a neighbourhood of zero, as is also
immediate from $t\geq a>0$.
For $a<x<b$, the real principal value of the arcsine transform
is zero, because $R(x+i0)$ is purely imaginary. This limiting
principal value also follows directly by subtracting the local
value of the smooth interior density in its Cauchy integral.
Consequently ECL10 gives
\[
\operatorname{PV}\int\frac{d\sigma_0(t)}{x-t}=-\frac1{2x}.
\]
Differentiating the logarithmic potential on each interior
subinterval is valid by that same local subtraction; the endpoint
parts are integrable and are a positive distance away.
It follows that
$-\log x-2\int\log|x-t|\,d\sigma_0(t)$ is constant on $(a,b)$.
Subtract this identity from ECL8. For
$\sigma_1=\sigma-\sigma_0$, the logarithmic potential
$\int\log|x-t|\,d\sigma_1(t)$ is constant on $(a,b)$.
Since $\sigma_1$ has zero mass and no endpoint atoms, and
$|\sigma_1|\leq C\nu$, absolute integrability proved after ECL5 gives
\[
-\iint\log|x-t|\,d\sigma_1(x)d\sigma_1(t)=0.
\]
The exact zero-mass energy argument EIQ20--22, including its
Fourier-uniqueness proof, forces $\sigma_1=0$. This establishes
the claimed derivative as a measure, with every endpoint
contribution in ECL7 retained.

\subsection{The moment and energy formulas}

Integrate ECL8 against $\nu$. By ECL5 and $\sigma([a,b])=0$,
the iterated logarithmic term is zero. Therefore
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL11}
\dot\ell=-2\log w.
\end{equation}
All exchanges again have the absolute integrability established
after ECL5; the equality ECL8 holds $\nu$-almost everywhere.
Using ECL9 with $f(x)=\log x$, and then ECL4, proves
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL12}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathcal L_\alpha
=\frac12\int\left(1-\frac z x\right)\log x\,d\nu(x)
=2\log w-\log z=\log(w^2/z).}
\end{equation}

Write $I=\iint\log|x-t|\,d\mu(x)d\mu(t)$, with the original
unscaled logarithmic kernel. The EIQ31 dilation identity is
$\beta\int\sqrt{x}\,d\mu=2(\alpha+1)$.
Integration of the Euler equality against $\mu$ gives
$\ell=\int V\,d\mu-2I$. Combining this with
$F=I-\int V\,d\mu$ yields the exact identity
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL13}
\ell=-2F-2(\alpha+1)+\alpha\mathcal L.
\end{equation}
EIQ30 proves $F_\alpha=\mathcal L$. Differentiating ECL13 at
fixed $\beta$, with the differentiability just proved, gives
$\dot\ell=-\mathcal L-2+\alpha\mathcal L_\alpha$.
Substituting ECL11--12 now proves, without an integration
constant or a limiting parameter choice,
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL14}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathcal L=2(\alpha+1)\log w-\alpha\log z-2,\qquad
F=-\frac{\ell}{2}-(\alpha+1)+\frac{\alpha\mathcal L}{2}.}
\end{equation}
Equations ECL6, ECL12, and ECL14 therefore evaluate the same
Robin constant, logarithmic moment, and variational energy as
the original EIQ integrals.

\subsection{The original outer coefficient and the two scale conventions}

The coefficient of OFV29 is defined by its full original expression
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL15}
\mathfrak C_{\partial}
=4\log(4/\vartheta)-10
-2F(2,\vartheta)+4\mathcal L(2,\vartheta),
\qquad \vartheta=\pi/2.
\end{equation}
To express this same number at the original EIQ limiting potential
$V_{2,\pi}(x)=\pi\sqrt{x}-2\log x$, retain the actual dilation
$x\mapsto(\pi/\beta)^2x$ of EIQ32. Its effect on the interaction,
potential integral, and moment is, respectively,
\[
\begin{split}
I(\alpha,\beta)&=I(\alpha,\pi)+2\log(\pi/\beta),\\
\int V_{\alpha,\beta}\,d\mu_{\alpha,\beta}
&=\int V_{\alpha,\pi}\,d\mu_{\alpha,\pi}
   -2\alpha\log(\pi/\beta),\\
\mathcal L(\alpha,\beta)&=\mathcal L(\alpha,\pi)
   +2\log(\pi/\beta).
\end{split}
\]
Thus $F(\alpha,\beta)=F(\alpha,\pi)
+2(\alpha+1)\log(\pi/\beta)$, with its stated sign.
At $\alpha=2$ the added coefficient in ECL15 is
$(4-12+8)\log(\pi/\beta)=0$. It follows exactly that
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL16}
c_{\partial}:=\mathfrak C_{\partial}
=4\log(4/\pi)-10-2F(2,\pi)+4\mathcal L(2,\pi).
\end{equation}
For the rest of this subsection $u,v,w,z,c_{\mathrm{cap}}$ are
precisely their ECL2--3 values at $(\alpha,\beta)=(2,\pi)$.
They therefore satisfy $uK(k)=2$ and $vE(k)=4$.
Substitution of the established formulas, with their constants,
gives
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL17}
\boxed{\displaystyle
c_{\partial}
=4\log(4/\pi)+8\log w-4\log z-2\log c_{\mathrm{cap}}.}
\end{equation}
For example, ECL6 gives
$\ell=8-4\log w-2\log c_{\mathrm{cap}}$, ECL14 gives
$\mathcal L=6\log w-2\log z-2$, and ECL13 gives
$F=-\ell/2-3+\mathcal L$. In ECL16 these yield
$4\log(4/\pi)-4+\ell+2\mathcal L$, in which the constant
terms are exactly $-4+8-4=0$, proving ECL17.
Equivalently $F(2,\pi)=I(2,\pi)-6+2\mathcal L(2,\pi)$
in ECL16 gives
$c_{\partial}=4\log(4/\pi)+2-2I(2,\pi)$,
which is precisely the OFV31 energy convention.
This section evaluates this coefficient. It does not assign it
as the leading coefficient of a separate observation residual
or a separate kernel determinant.

\subsection{An integer-only outward certificate}

The accompanying \texttt{certify\_closed\_equilibrium.py} proves
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL18}
\boxed{21.66851180520<16c_{\partial}<21.66851180521.}
\end{equation}
The receipt stores every interval endpoint as an integer divided by
$S=10^{70}$, including the original endpoints and all quantities in
ECL17. The script uses no floating-point inputs or numerical quadrature.
The following argument proves the correctness of its finite algorithm.

Each stored interval $[A,B]$ denotes $[A/S,B/S]$. Addition and
subtraction are exact. For multiplication, the smallest and largest
of the four endpoint products are divided by $S$ and rounded
respectively down and up. For division by an interval excluding zero,
the four ratios of endpoint integers, multiplied by $S$, are
rounded outwards. Their extrema enclose every possible product or
quotient because these operations attain their extrema at corners
of their rectangular domains. The rounding uses integer floor
division and $\lceil n/d\rceil=-\lfloor-n/d\rfloor$ after making
$d>0$. These formulas apply to negative numerators as well.
Consequently induction proves containment after every arithmetic
operation, without a probabilistic or floating-point error estimate.

Let $h=u/v$ in this paragraph only; this is a scalar ratio and
does not replace the density function $h(x)$ of EIQ10. Put
$m_e=1-h^2$, so $k=\sqrt{m_e}$. Define
$b_n=\binom{2n}{n}/4^n$ and $t_n=b_n^2m_e^n$.
Expansion of the binomial series, followed by its uniformly
convergent integration for $m_e<1$, proves
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL19}
\frac{2K(\sqrt{m_e})}{\pi}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t_n,\qquad
\frac{2E(\sqrt{m_e})}{\pi}
=1-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{t_n}{2n-1},\qquad
\frac{t_n}{t_{n-1}}=m_e\frac{(2n-1)^2}{4n^2}.
\end{equation}
Indeed integration by parts gives
$\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2n}\theta\,d\theta=(\pi/2)b_n$;
the two binomial coefficients are $b_n$ and
$-b_n/(2n-1)$ for $n\geq1$. Since
$t_{n+1}/t_n\leq m_e$, the positive omitted tails after
indices $0,\ldots,N-1$ have the rigorous bounds
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL20}
0\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}t_n\leq\frac{t_N}{1-m_e},
\qquad
0\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\frac{t_n}{2n-1}
\leq\frac{t_N}{(2N-1)(1-m_e)}.
\end{equation}
The script takes $N=3200$, applies the recurrence with outward
rounding at each step, and adds these tails with their proper
signs. They remain valid for interval values of $m_e$ by the
interval arithmetic just proved.

The two exact terminating decimals used for the root bracket are
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL21}
\begin{split}
h_-&=0.160101670957718355884567453115,\\
h_+&=0.160101670957718355884567453116.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Let $K_b=2K/\pi$, $E_b=2E/\pi$ as in ECL19.
The receipt gives strictly negative upper bound for
$2h_-K_b(1-h_-^2)-E_b(1-h_-^2)$ and strictly positive lower
bound for the corresponding expression at $h_+$.
Here the arguments of $K_b,E_b$ denote $m_e$ as defined by
their series, not the modulus argument of EIQ2.
The common factor $\pi/2>0$ in the original equation cancels
exactly. EIQ6 proves that $hK(\sqrt{1-h^2})/
E(\sqrt{1-h^2})$ increases strictly from zero to one as
$h$ increases from zero to one. Thus ECL21 encloses the
unique root of the original endpoint equation
$hK(\sqrt{1-h^2})=E(\sqrt{1-h^2})/2$.

The value of $\pi$ is enclosed independently by
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL22}
\pi=16\arctan(1/5)-4\arctan(1/239).
\end{equation}
To prove the identity, if $\theta=\arctan(1/5)$, then
$\tan(2\theta)=5/12$ and $\tan(4\theta)=120/119$.
The subtraction formula gives
$\tan(4\theta-\arctan(1/239))=1$.
This angle lies in $(0,\pi/2)$:
$4\theta<4/5<1<\pi/2$, while
$4\theta\geq4(1/5)/(1+1/25)>1/239$.
The inequality $\pi/2>1$ follows from
$\arctan1=\int_0^1(1+t^2)^{-1}dt>1/2$.
Consequently the angle is $\pi/4$, proving ECL22.
Each arctangent is computed from its alternating series with
128 terms. The first omitted term bounds the absolute remainder;
its sign is positive for this even number of terms. Outward
arithmetic and that signed tail therefore enclose the exact $\pi$.

For every positive logarithm argument $x$ put $t=(x-1)/(x+1)$.
The geometric series, integrated on the segment from zero to $t$,
gives
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL23}
\log x=2\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}\frac{t^{2n+1}}{2n+1}
+R_M,\qquad
|R_M|\leq
\frac{2|t|^{2M+1}}{(2M+1)(1-|t|^2)}.
\end{equation}
The script takes $M=256$. For an interval argument it uses
the maximum absolute endpoint of the enclosing $t$ interval
in this tail bound and adds a symmetric outward tail. Thus
ECL23 encloses the logarithm throughout the argument interval.

The enclosed original endpoint values are computed in order by
\[
\begin{gathered}
K=(\pi/2)K_b,\qquad E=(\pi/2)E_b,\qquad v=4/E,\qquad u=hv,\\
w=(u+v)/2,\qquad z=uv,\qquad c_{\mathrm{cap}}=(v^2-u^2)/4.
\end{gathered}
\]
The script checks $0<u<v$, positive capacity, strict endpoint
signs, and that every division interval excludes zero. It applies
ECL23 to the four arguments $4/\pi,w,z,c_{\mathrm{cap}}$
and evaluates the full expression ECL17 with its original
coefficients. Integer comparison of the final bounds proves
the stronger enclosure
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL24}
\begin{split}
21.668511805204674126143836401790042003
&<16c_{\partial}\\
&<21.668511805204674126143836403323660355.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The decimal endpoints in ECL24 are rounded outwards from the
stored integer endpoints. Their comparison with the exact
terminating decimals in ECL18 proves ECL18.

The same operations also enclose the endpoint expression
\begin{equation}\tag{ECL25}
\frac18\log(w^2/z)-\frac14\log2
\ \in\ (-0.080453400358,\,-0.080453400357).
\end{equation}
This is an evaluation of this explicit expression. If it is used
as an earlier approximation centre, its previously specified
error radius is not changed by this calculation.
The ordinary and optimized Python executions produce identical
JSON receipts. All mathematical checks use explicit exceptions,
so disabling Python assertions does not disable any certificate
condition. The receipt includes the script hash, integer endpoint
signs, elliptic-tail bounds, every resulting interval, and the
strict target comparisons.

\section{The closed coefficient in the original QGQ return centre}
\label{sec:EQR}

Retain the complete original QGQ1--9 theorem and its full provider
\texttt{02\_FULL\_GAMMA\_PROOFS.tex}, section
\emph{The finite original \(m=1\) return bound at the \(q\) scale}.
Retain its actual Gamma functional $W_k$, finite centre
$\mathcal V^{\rm c}_{q,l}$, constants $\mathcal C_\Gamma,A,M,C$,
and its original packet
\[
m=1,\qquad k=4l+1,\qquad q=(4l+2)^2,\qquad n=q/2,\qquad l\geq1.
\]
The support and endpoint values $u,v,w,z$ in this section are
exactly those of ECL2--3 at $(\alpha,\beta)=(2,\pi)$.
In particular $uK(k_{\rm ell})=2$, $vE(k_{\rm ell})=4$, and
$k_{\rm ell}^2=1-u^2/v^2$; the subscript distinguishes this
elliptic modulus from the original integer packet degree $k$.
No endpoint or original degree is changed by this notation.

QGQ4 defines, for the original EIQ logarithmic moment,
$\mathcal L_1=\partial_\alpha\mathcal L(2,\pi)$ and
$\mathcal C_\Gamma^{[q,1]}=\mathcal L_1/8-(\log2)/4$.
The derivative proved in ECL12 is a derivative of precisely
this same moment, at fixed original $\beta$. Therefore the
following is an exact identification of the original centre:
\begin{equation}\tag{EQR1}
\boxed{\begin{gathered}
\mathcal L_1=\log(w^2/z),\qquad
\mathcal C_\Gamma^{[q,1]}
=\frac18\log(w^2/z)-\frac14\log2,\\
-0.080453400358
<\mathcal C_\Gamma^{[q,1]}
<-0.080453400357.
\end{gathered}}
\end{equation}
The first two equalities follow by substitution into the literal
QGQ4 definition; there is no new centre or change of energy
convention. The last line is consequently the strict outward
integer enclosure ECL25 for that exact original centre. Its
proof and stored integer endpoints are those of ECL19--25.

The original finite-centre theorem QGQ8 now gives
\begin{equation}\tag{EQR2}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\frac{\mathcal V^{\rm c}_{q,l}-lq\mathcal C_\Gamma}{q}
\longrightarrow
\frac18\log(w^2/z)-\frac14\log2
\qquad(l\longrightarrow\infty).}
\end{equation}
To check the connection to the original finite calculation
explicitly, QGQ7 retains its first term
$(2\mathcal L_1-4\log2)l^2$ and all its other original terms.
The complete termwise estimates following QGQ7 bound their
sum by $O(l)$; in particular its actual remainder
$\zeta_{q,l}$ is $O(l)$ by QGQ6. On the literal packet,
$l^2/q\to1/16$ and $l/q\to0$. Thus dividing that same identity
by $q$ gives
$(2\mathcal L_1-4\log2)/16=\mathcal L_1/8-(\log2)/4$.
Substitution of EQR1 proves EQR2 with the original finite centre,
source, low endpoint, and rank correction intact.

The original error radius remains the following nonzero number:
\begin{equation}\tag{EQR3}
\boxed{\displaystyle
\limsup_{l\to\infty}
\left|
\frac{W_k-lq\mathcal C_\Gamma}{q}
-\left(\frac18\log(w^2/z)-\frac14\log2\right)
\right|
\leq\frac A{\sqrt2},
\qquad A=M/2+2C>0.}
\end{equation}
Here $M$ and $C$ retain their complete original QGT12 and QGT9
definitions. To verify the transfer, retain the finite QGT22
inequality used in QGQ9:
\[
-p^-_{q,l}-\mathcal U_{q,l}-E_{q,l}
\leq W_k-\mathcal V^{\rm c}_{q,l}
\leq p^+_{q,l}+\mathcal U_{q,l}+T_{q,l}+E_{q,l}.
\]
QGQ1--2 proves that each of
$p^-_{q,l}/q,p^+_{q,l}/q,T_{q,l}/q,E_{q,l}/q$ tends to zero,
while $\mathcal U_{q,l}/q\to A/\sqrt2$. Hence the difference
$(W_k-\mathcal V^{\rm c}_{q,l})/q$ is bounded below by
$-A/\sqrt2-o(1)$ and above by $A/\sqrt2+o(1)$.
Adding the convergent expression in EQR2, and using the triangle
inequality, proves EQR3. Both unequal finite errors remain in the
displayed original inequality; their passage to the same limiting
radius does not replace that finite interval by a new one.

The radius is strictly positive from the original definitions
themselves. QGT9 gives $C\geq0$, and QGT12 gives
\[
M=\max_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal K}
|\partial_\alpha\mathcal L(\alpha,\beta)|
\geq\partial_\alpha\mathcal L(2,\pi)=\log(w^2/z)>0,
\]
because $(2,\pi)\in\mathcal K$ and
$w^2/z=1+(v-u)^2/(4uv)>1$ by $0<u<v$.
Consequently $A=M/2+2C>0$. This proves that the retained
nonzero radius has not disappeared upon evaluating its centre.
The only convergence asserted in EQR2 concerns the original
finite centre. Neither EQR1 nor EQR3 asserts a limit for the
actual quantity $(W_k-lq\mathcal C_\Gamma)/q$.
